Teleperformance · TEP-FR · the new Memo conveyor's card of 11 November 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Teleperformance

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 48,063 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

–not yet rated

Memo topic 1, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external environment is a regime of structural price deflation for Teleperformance's core. AI and offshoring lower what clients pay per unit of work; clients keep the productivity; competitors defend volume with discounts; labour costs in the hubs rise under statute; and the technology that would let Teleperformance capture value is owned by its suppliers. The disruption reached the reported numbers in 2026 (Trust & Safety, and the guidance cut of 2026-11-05), which is why the picture is worse than 12-18 months ago, when the damage could still be explained as isolated. The real tailwinds are share consolidation, arithmetic (the Trust & Safety base effect), currency in 2027 and private-market demand for Specialized Services; none grows the revenue pool. Steer for the model: group organic growth of about -2% in 2026, about 0% in 2027 and +0.5% to +1.5% in 2028, with price and mix as the swing factor rather than volume. Assume no cyclical recovery premium. Model a currency tailwind in 2027 explicitly, and keep a step-down scenario for a second vertical.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0220C-0540C-0740C-0944C-3520C-3930
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

F2054th of 55

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Competitive intensity is rising, and it bites through price rather than through the number of bidders. Share is concentrating at the top, but discounting is spreading and a new class of technology-first competitors prices against Teleperformance's cost base, not just its margin. The direction is against the company.

Reasoning.

  1. Discounting is spreading. The share of enterprise deals with upfront discounts rose from 10-15% in 2025 to 15-25% by mid-2026 (expert call). Concentrix is "increasingly offering upfront discounts" in competitive rebids. Its management calls commodity customer-experience work "highly competitive", with peers "chasing volume with minimal regard for contract profitability". Former Teleperformance directors say the industry has "returned to a price-led environment due to AI".
  2. New competitive vectors. IT-services firms (HCL, Wipro, Infosys, Capgemini) sell automated customer care. Software vendors bill on outcomes (Zendesk's AI agents; Genesys, with about USD 2.9 billion of annual recurring revenue, building agentic orchestration). AI-native and agile operators are scaling: Ibex with Sierra; TaskUs with Decagon and Regal; Concentrix now earns 50% of revenue from contracts won since AI arrived. Their cost per transaction can be a quarter of a human-delivered one (USD 0.50 against USD 2 per invoice in one client's example).
  3. Offsets. Clients are cutting their vendor panels in favour of scaled, global, compliant providers (Morgan Stanley). Weak regional players such as Atento are losing clients. Private buyers have tried to take sub-scale peers (TaskUs, TTEC) private.
  4. What changed in the last six months. Concentrix booked a second goodwill impairment (USD 1.05 billion in Q3 2026, after USD 1.52 billion in FY2025), its revenue fell 1.2% in Q3, and it has an activist on the register (Impactive, USD 266.7 million). A wounded number two may either price harder to defend volume or turn disciplined under activist pressure. Its rebid behaviour so far points to the first.

For the model. Do not let consolidation stand in for pricing power. Model share gains in volume, offset by a continuing price give-back at renewal.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

D4037th of 55

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Inputs are a moderate net headwind in 2026. Into 2027 they are mixed: wage inflation in the hubs and a new, rising compute cost persist, but delivery currencies that are expected to weaken give an exchange-rate tailwind on costs once the twelve-month hedges roll off. Supplier concentration is rising in technology, a new structural dependency.

Reasoning.

  1. Labour (personnel is 65-70% of Core Services revenue). See C-04 for hub wage inflation. Agent attrition was 66-68% in FY2024 against 45-55% at the best competitors (and an industry norm of 35-45% in one source). Replacing an agent costs USD 80-100. The HaireX recruiting tool (+41% hiring conversion, +31% successful hires) and training cut to two weeks reduce these costs.
  2. Technology. Bernstein flags "escalating compute and AI token costs" that BPOs absorb while clients ask for lower prices. Deutsche Bank says Teleperformance cannot earn meaningful reseller fees from Microsoft, OpenAI and similar firms, which sell directly to enterprises. This cost line did not exist in the labour-only model, and the company does not own the technology.
  3. Real estate and capital expenditure. 35-40% of the workforce works from home. Net capital expenditure fell to 1.9% of revenue in H1 2026, from over 5% historically.
  4. Currency on costs. The Philippine peso is 25% of cost of goods sold against 1.4% of revenue; the Indian rupee is 15% against 4.5%. Forecasts point to weaker delivery currencies against the dollar: Deutsche Bank sees USD/PHP at 62 at end-2026 and Morgan Stanley expects the peso to underperform; USD/INR is forecast at 97-98.5; the Colombian peso is leading Latin American losses on Colombia's fiscal crisis. The risk report's sensitivities are that a 10% peso appreciation cuts EPS 3.6% and a 10% rupee appreciation cuts it 3.0%. A 5-10% weakening of both is therefore worth roughly +1.5% to +3.5% of EPS (my calculation, symmetric use of the report's sensitivities). Hedges cover about twelve months (PHP 34,245 million notional for 2026), so the benefit arrives with a lag, mainly in 2027.
  5. Translation. Morgan Stanley sees EUR/USD at 1.10 by mid-2027, a translation tailwind on the 32% of revenue billed in dollars; Deutsche Bank sees 1.13-1.17. Each one-cent move in USD/EUR is worth about EUR 20 million of revenue; a 1% move in the euro is worth EUR 8.3 million of pre-tax profit.

For the model. In 2027 net the currency tailwind against wage inflation and compute cost. Treat compute as a rising cost line that cannot be passed on.

C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

D4026th of 55

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Macro forces act at three levels. At the industry level, client budgets are being moved to AI projects. At the company level, currency works through both translation and the gap between billing currencies and delivery-cost currencies. At the market level, the discount rate and the French sovereign spread act on a leveraged, French-listed equity. It bites hardest at the company level: through currency on reported numbers, and through the discount rate on an equity that is only about 55% of enterprise value.

Reasoning.

  1. Industry. Morgan Stanley finds AI projects "crowding out traditional discretionary IT spend", delaying or cancelling outsourcing contracts. Expected IT-services spending growth is 1.8%.
  2. Company: currency. Translation cost EUR 165 million of H1 2026 revenue (about 3.3%) and EUR 362 million of FY2025 revenue, and took about 20 basis points off the FY2025 margin. The direction into 2027 is a modest tailwind (C-05).
  3. Market: the discount rate. The Fitness for Fighting digest (2026-11-06) reads risk-off beneath an index near its high: French ten-year yields above 5%, the French-German spread at 174 basis points, euro high yield 90 basis points wider since September. It rates France "Double Underweight" and Fragility "Double Underweight", and judges Teleperformance's fit "Mixed": a disposal project in a cracked credit market. Market capitalisation is EUR 4,251 million of an EUR 7,631 million enterprise value, so a 10% fall in enterprise value is about an 18% fall in the equity (my calculation).
  4. Interest cost. Net interest was EUR 282 million in FY2025 (FactSet, including leases). A EUR 1.2 billion bond in May 2026 extended average maturity to 4.5 years, so refinancing exposure is limited before 2028.
  5. Sector. Sector K.1 has no common cycle (APM note, 2026-10-22). At the Industry level, the fear that AI would replace people is clearing only "where results show little damage". Teleperformance's results show damage, so that relief has not reached it.

For the model. Run explicit sensitivities to EUR/USD, the peso and the rupee. In valuation, carry a discount-rate and country premium that is not company-specific.

C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–44

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Two dynamics matter most, and they pull in opposite directions. Positively, the gap between private and listed multiples supports the value of a Specialized Services sale. Negatively, the impairment cycle among peers now reaches Teleperformance's own balance sheet, because its market capitalisation has fallen below its mostly-goodwill book equity. A third, slower dynamic, consumer AI agents taking over the customer interface, threatens the whole intermediary role.

Reasoning.

  1. Private-market appetite (positive for the disposal). Listed European business-services companies trade at 9.1 times EV/EBITDA, against 12.0 times paid for unlisted ones since 2020 and an average 13.5 times with a 57% premium in take-outs since 2021 (Morgan Stanley, 2026-05-11). AlphaSense judges the window for private bids "open through 2027 unless public valuations recover". This favours a sale of LanguageLine or TLScontact at a multiple above the group's. Against it, wider credit spreads raise financing costs for leveraged buyers.
  2. Impairment cascade (negative). Concentrix took USD 1.52 billion and then USD 1.05 billion of goodwill impairments, driven by its depressed market capitalisation. Teleperformance already took EUR 97 million (PSG and Health Advocate) in FY2025. With market capitalisation below book equity (Reader's key, item 5), the FY2026 impairment test (results 2027-02-25) is a live risk. It would be non-cash, but a headline that confirms "value destroyed".
  3. Consumer AI agents (negative, slow). If agents talk to agents (Muse), the volume of human-handled interactions can fall, not just its price (the Wisdom Library's "The buying agent does not feel loyalty").
  4. Labour scrutiny of content moderation. The precedent is November 2022: a 34% fall in a day and a trading halt. Colombian authorities later closed the probe without findings.
  5. French political risk. Pernod Ricard's file notes Morgan Stanley's warning of a negative euro risk premium ahead of the spring 2027 presidential election. For Teleperformance this is a listing effect; its revenue is global.
  6. Activism at the main peer (Impactive at Concentrix) could push the industry towards price discipline or consolidation.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

F2055th of 55

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The threat is low from standard entrants, high from adjacent entrants and highest from orthogonal ones. The combined threat is high and rising.

Reasoning.

  1. Standard entrants (new labour-based BPOs): low. The barriers are scale, compliance, delivery in nearly 100 countries, and the capital to fund technology.
  2. Adjacent entrants: high. - IT services (HCL, Wipro, Infosys, Capgemini). Bernstein's "Services-as-Software" thesis: clients buy execution capacity rather than labour, and revenue decouples from headcount. - Customer-experience software vendors billing on outcomes (Zendesk; Genesys). - Hyperscalers, which own the platforms.
  3. Orthogonal entrants: highest. - AI-native agent firms: Sierra (partnered with Ibex), Decagon (with TaskUs), and also Parloa and Ema, Teleperformance's own partners. - Clients' in-house AI. - Consumer agents (Muse). - One client example: USD 0.50 against USD 2 per invoice.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

F3043rd of 55

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Suppliers are a source of risk. Labour is scarce and getting dearer in the hubs, and technology suppliers have rising bargaining power and rising prices. Real estate is the only easing input. The equity stakes in AI firms give modest support (reseller rights) but not control.

Reasoning. Labour: see C-05. Technology: Salesforce is managed centrally through the US branch; a single Microsoft ERP; Azure and AWS for hosting, so the company is "highly exposed to third-party hosting pricing"; token costs are rising. External expenses were EUR 1,241 million in FY2025. AI partners: Sanas, Ema and Parloa (minority stakes; exclusive resale), though Bernstein judges the stakes largely unnecessary. Real estate: working from home cuts the second-largest cost line after salaries.

1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0120C-0315C-0438C-0838C-3225C-3738C-4330
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

F2055th of 55

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Demand for Teleperformance's core product, human-delivered customer interactions billed by the hour or the seat, is in structural decline in its simplest workflows. That decline accelerated over the last six months and sits on top of a milder cyclical softness in client budgets. Visibility is under twelve months and getting worse, so there is no cyclical swing with two or more years of visibility to model.

Reasoning.

  1. Structural, and it arrived as a step. Trust & Safety fell from 8.0% of revenue (FY2025) to 5.5% (nine months of 2026), "much faster than corporate planners originally anticipated", and caused half of the November guidance cut. By my calculation it is falling about 35-40% a year. A large client automated a whole vertical inside a few quarters. The lesson for modelling is that the next loss can also come as a step, in another vertical.
  2. The rest of the core is not collapsing; its price is falling. Excluding Trust & Safety, the group grew +1.7% in H1 2026 and +2.0% in Q3. By my calculation Core Services excluding Trust & Safety grew about +2.5% to +3%. Growth is coming from back-office work, financial services, outcome-based contracts and data services for AI (double-digit, from a small base). Revenue per unit of work is falling: offshoring takes about 150 basis points a year off organic growth, renewals carry 15-25% discounts, clients ask for 5-10% "automation discounts", and contracts renew at lower absolute spend.
  3. The cyclical part is real but secondary. Expected growth in IT-services spending is 1.8% (Q2 2026 CIO survey, from 2.0%). Discretionary spending by US technology and retail clients has low visibility, and US healthcare volumes are lighter (Medicaid membership down mid-to-high single digits). None of this supports a two-year cyclical recovery case.
  4. Visibility is short and getting shorter. Expert calls (a source class AlphaSense rates low in confidence) report contracts moving from three-to-five-year terms to annual renewals. The "sequential improvement" of Q2 was followed by a Q3 miss and a guide implying a weaker Q4 (about -3.5% at the midpoint, my calculation).
  5. Compared with the last 12-18 months, worse. In 2025 the problem looked specific: a lost visa contract, LanguageLine volumes hit by US immigration policy, offshoring. In 2026 the first large, explicitly AI-driven volume loss reached the reported numbers.

For the model. Model organic growth as two engines. Group excluding Trust & Safety: about +1% to +2%, with offshoring and price give-back already inside that figure. Trust & Safety: falling 30-40% a year on a shrinking weight, a drag of about -3.4 points in 2026, about -1.5 to -2 points in 2027 and about -1 point in 2028. Together that gives group organic growth of about 0% in 2027 and +0.5% to +1.5% in 2028: near consensus, and far below the 4-6% target for 2028.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

F1554th of 55

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Generative and agentic AI is strengthening against the core seat-based model and has moved from narrative to the reported numbers. The counter-evidence slows the disruption; it does not disprove it. The net effect on the company is clearly negative: it lands on about 85% of revenue (Core Services) and first on the highest-margin seats, while the positive effects reach only small lines.

Reasoning.

  1. Evidence it is strengthening. Trust & Safety is down about 35-40% a year (C-01). Meta cut TaskUs's revenue by 22% in Q2 2026 as it automated moderation. Meta launched its consumer agent Muse on 2026-09-08, which Morgan Stanley says "reignited the AI disruption debate" and creates "credible disruption risk". Meta, Sprint, GoDaddy and Verizon build their own tools and require their vendors to use them. Clients prioritising in-house AI have cut BPO spending 5-11%. Jefferies calls voice the "final frontier", and voice is 64% of Teleperformance's interactions. Studies put 20-50% of standard volumes at risk over a few years, and 15-20% within 12-24 months.
  2. Counter-evidence. Klarna re-hired human agents in May 2025 after automation hurt satisfaction. Clients are returning to "human-in-the-loop" models, and regulated and complex workflows resist. Article 50 of the EU AI Act (in force 2026-08-02) requires users to be told when they are dealing with AI. A Gartner survey finds 54% of consumers trust humans more. All of these slow adoption; none reverses it.
  3. How it acts on the company. It hits price before volume, because clients take the productivity gain at renewal. It hits the highest-margin core seats first (Trust & Safety). It creates small new demand pools: data services for AI (double-digit growth, size undisclosed), TP.ai (over 1,400 projects; 35% of clients), and "AI safety" (the renamed Trust & Safety unit).
  4. Peer read-across. At Concentrix, 50% of revenue now comes from AI-era contracts while total revenue still falls (-1.2% in Q3 2026). New business is winning, but not enough to cover the erosion of legacy work.

For the model. Treat disruption as a recurring step risk to volumes in single verticals and a continuous drag on price. The positive lines are too small to offset this in 2027-2028.

C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

F3844th of 55

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Substantial regulatory change is under way. It is mainly a cost headwind in the delivery hubs, mostly not passed on to clients, with a small offset where European AI and data rules slow automation and favour compliant, large providers. One tail risk, a US tax on offshored services, is low in probability and large in effect.

Reasoning.

  1. Colombia. Law 2466 of 2025 limits fixed-term contracts and raises night-shift surcharges (from 7 pm). The standard week fell from 44 to 42 hours in July 2026. The minimum wage rose 23% in nominal terms for 2026 (about 17% in real terms). Colombia is 4.9% of revenue and historically about 10% of headcount. The Americas core margin fell to 9.4%.
  2. Philippines. The Metro Manila floor is PHP 645 a day; regional boards are adding increases (PHP 42 a day in Central Visayas from October 2026); a PHP 100 nationwide rise has been proposed. The peso is 25% of group cost of goods sold.
  3. India and Egypt. India's four Labour Codes (in force late 2025) define wages as at least 50% of pay, raising benefit liabilities. The Digital Personal Data Protection Act is fully enforceable from 2027-05-13 (penalties up to ₹250 crore). Egypt set a minimum wage of EGP 7,000 a month and raised social-insurance bases.
  4. United States. The proposed HIRE Act would put a non-deductible 25% tax on payments to foreign entities for services to US consumers. India is about 6% of group revenue, and US-facing nearshore and offshore work is far larger. Bernstein expects pricing pressure rather than onshoring.
  5. Mild offsets. The EU AI Act transparency rule, GDPR, DORA and HIPAA raise the compliance bar, which favours large certified operators and slows agentic substitution in Europe.
  6. Pass-through. Some Latin American contracts carry cost-of-living clauses: Latin American operations grew 7% in local currency on those clauses. The sector rule of thumb holds, though: indexation passes on wage inflation, not statutory labour costs such as shorter hours or surcharges.

For the model. Add 2026-27 cost pressure in the Americas and the Philippines, recovered only in part. Keep the US tax as a scenario, not a base case.

C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

F3852nd of 55

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. No industry or sector driver is accelerating demand for the company's revenue. The drivers that are moving (vendor consolidation, offshoring, compliance complexity, AI data services) speed up share, mix or volume while slowing or deflating revenue.

Reasoning.

  1. Outsourcing penetration, the historical engine: only 32% of customer care is outsourced (60-65% of capacity is in-house). The runway exists, but clients now automate in-house instead of outsourcing. The sector rule is that clients claim most automation savings themselves.
  2. Vendor consolidation is a real tailwind to share, worth perhaps a point of organic growth to the largest providers (my judgement). It does not grow the market.
  3. Offshoring speeds up volumes to India, South Africa and Egypt, where billing rates are 20-30% lower (Morgan Stanley elsewhere says India's are 70% lower). It is margin-accretive and revenue-deflationary.
  4. Regulated complexity (financial services and insurance 17% of revenue, healthcare 7% of Core Services) supports demand for compliant scale. It is stable, not accelerating.
  5. AI data services and implementation: double-digit growth from a small base. Its size is not disclosed anywhere in the research.
  6. The market for customer-experience services grows 1-3% a year (expert call); Ambit projects a 1.6% compound rate to 2027.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

F2554th of 55

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Customers are rationalising, automating and demanding a share of the productivity gains. They support the thesis only through vendor consolidation, long relationships and the stickiness of complex, regulated work. That support is fragile because it is tested at every renewal, and renewals are getting shorter and cheaper.

Reasoning.

  1. Supportive. More than 1,500 clients. The largest is 6% of revenue, the top five 22%, the top ten 31%, the top twenty 40%. The top 100 clients have stayed 14 years on average. Complex tasks move onshore to keep quality. Financial services, healthcare and travel are sticky verticals.
  2. Not supportive. - Renewals at lower spend and shorter terms. - Clients build their own tools and require vendors to use them. - BPO spending is down 5-11% at clients prioritising in-house AI; one client's spending with Concentrix fell 7-8%. - Corporate buyers want "productivity-sharing commitments". - Mid-sized clients report weak service and missed service levels because of attrition.
  3. Fragility. Concentration is low by client but high by vertical behaviour: technology and social-media clients automated Trust & Safety together.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

F3845th of 55

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. On balance regulators and politicians hurt, through labour costs in the hubs and the US offshoring tax risk. They help a little, through compliance burdens that favour scale and slow AI substitution in Europe. The position is moderately fragile: a single law (a US offshoring tax, or a nationwide Philippine wage rise) could move the margin by a point or more.

Reasoning. Details are in C-04. Helpful: GDPR, HIPAA, DORA, transparency under the EU AI Act, and ISO 27701 certifications. Hurtful: Colombia (Law 2466, the 42-hour week, a 23% minimum-wage rise), the Philippines (wage boards), India (the Labour Codes and the data-protection law), and the US HIRE Act proposal. Precedent: Colombia's 2022 labour probe moved the shares 34% in a day and was later closed without findings.

C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

F3052nd of 55

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. The addressable market is large relative to the company: about EUR 100 billion of outsourced customer experience, implied by a 10% share, inside a customer-care spend about three times that size. It allows share gains but little growth. The value of that market per unit of work is deflating, so realistic mid-term organic growth is about 0-2% a year, not the 4-6% targeted.

Reasoning.

  1. Size. EUR 10.2 billion at about 10% share implies an outsourced market of about EUR 100 billion (my calculation). 32% of customer care is outsourced.
  2. Growth. 1-3% a year (expert call); a 1.6% compound rate to 2027 (Ambit).
  3. Shrinking pools. 20-50% of standard volumes are automatable. Content moderation is being automated now. Voice is next.
  4. Growing pools. AI data services, AI operations and safety, outcome-based back-office work, consulting. Each is growing but small; none is sized in the research.
  5. The distinction that matters for the model. The TAM in volume may hold, while the TAM in revenue shrinks through price and offshoring.
1c

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0628C-1020
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

F2852nd of 55

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. The organic cycle is at or near its low, but it is not turning. The apparent turn in Q2 2026 proved false, and the guidance implies a weaker Q4. From Q1 2027 a mechanical improvement in the rate of change is likely as Trust & Safety laps its steepest declines, but organic growth itself is likely to stay near zero through H1 2027.

Reasoning.

  1. Trajectory. Organic growth averaged 8.0% from 2010 to 2019 and reached +11.6% in 2020. The 2023 pro forma target was cut from 10% to 6%, and FY2024 ended near 2%. FY2025 was +1.0%. In 2026: Q1 -2.2%, Q2 -1.2%, Q3 -1.2%, with Q4 implied at about -1.5% to -5.5% (my calculation).
  2. What could turn it. The Trust & Safety base effect: its weight falls from about 8% to perhaps 4% by end-2027 (my estimate), so its drag shrinks even if it keeps falling 30-40% a year. Offshoring drag continues at about 150 basis points a year but stops getting worse. Outcome-based and back-office work grew double-digit on a two-quarter stack. The sales-force overhaul in H1 2026 produced double-digit bookings growth, and a large US bank contract was ramping in H2 2026.
  3. What could prevent it. Price deflation at renewal, consumer and enterprise AI agents (Muse), and a second "Trust & Safety-like" step in another vertical.
  4. Peer read-across. Concentrix grew 1.9% organically in Q1 2026, guided 1-2% for Q2, and reported revenue -1.2% for Q3. The cycle is industry-wide, not peculiar to Teleperformance.

For the model. Q4 2026 and Q1 2027 at about -2% to -3%, then improving to about 0% by H2 2027. That is a base-effect recovery, not a demand recovery.

C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

F2053rd of 55

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. The external picture is a strong, broad headwind with narrow tailwinds, and it has worsened over the last six months as disruption moved into the reported numbers. Five of six value-chain forces push against the core. The tailwinds are share-shifting, arithmetic or financial rather than demand-driven.

The thematic drivers in turn.

  1. Sector K.1, "AI savings turned into tender wins by the largest outsourcers" (rated Early): the note names Teleperformance on the losing side, as "work billed by the hour of a customer-service agent". Headwind.
  2. Sector K.1, "private bids and buybacks at low listed multiples": a tailwind to the value of the Specialized assets. Teleperformance is not running a buyback, so the "10% buyback yield" in the Sector and Industry digests is a potential, not a current fact. Mild tailwind.
  3. Sector K.1, "how each contract shares out a wage rise": Teleperformance's per-hour and per-seat contracts pass productivity to clients, and statutory costs are not recovered. Headwind.
  4. Sector K.1, "a fear that touches a small part of revenue can set the whole multiple": here the fear touches most of the revenue (voice is 64% of interactions), so a multiple recovery on unchanged earnings is unlikely. Headwind.
  5. Industry K, "the AI fear clearing where results show little damage": it is not clearing for Teleperformance (Q3 guidance cut). The Industry's 2026-10-23 summary names it among the "sellers of hours or data" that lose. Headwind.
  6. Industry K, "how the client pays decides who keeps an automation saving": about 85% of pricing is per seat or per hour, so the saving is handed back. Headwind.

By level. Value chain: customers (strong headwind), competitors (headwind), disruptors (strong headwind), regulators (moderate headwind), suppliers (moderate headwind, with a currency tailwind into 2027). Industry: 1-3% growth, consolidation a moderate tailwind to share. Sector and Industry tilts: both Neutral in the Fitness for Fighting table. Market: France and Fragility both "Double Underweight", a valuation headwind.

The tailwinds, all narrow: vendor consolidation; the Trust & Safety base effect from 2027; weaker delivery currencies and a stronger dollar in 2027; private-market demand for specialised assets.

Memo topic 2, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The internal change is genuine, large and well led at the top, and it has stopped the old capital-allocation errors. It has not yet reached the profit and loss account beyond a held margin, and the first test of the new team's forecasting failed. It is a self-help plan of cost reduction and capital management inside a deflating value chain, not a plan that changes what clients pay. Steer for the model. Fully credit the mechanical items: tax below 30%, the second half of the savings in 2027, interest savings from deleveraging, and a flat EUR 4.50-4.80 dividend. Assume only about one third of the gross savings is retained in the margin. Treat the Specialized Services disposal as a priced option with explicit inputs, not a base case. Pencil in a mid-term reset (and possibly a new restructuring charge) with the FY2026 results on 2027-02-25.

Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The risk profile is dominated by an engine that has decelerated deeply under accelerating disruption, with sudden step risks (one large client, one hub event, one reset) layered on top. The downside to a fair trough is 22-30%, and about 40% if the earnings basis is re-based. C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

C+6212th of 55

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Standards are improving in cost discipline, working capital, tax, financing and governance. They are not improving in commercial execution, forecasting or culture. The improvement is real where management controls the lever and absent where the client does.

Reasoning.

  1. Improving. - Overheads were held: the H1 2026 margin beat consensus by 20 basis points, and the Specialized Services margin rose 330 basis points to 33.0%. - Net capital expenditure was cut to 1.9% of revenue (EUR 94 million against EUR 115 million). - The working-capital outflow fell to EUR 29 million from EUR 122 million. - The effective tax rate fell to 29.0% from 33.1% as Majorel entities were merged. - A EUR 1.2 billion bond extended average maturity to 4.5 years from 3. - Governance: chairman and chief executive separated, the board refreshed, an AI and Innovation Committee set up, regional employee boards promised by end-2026. - Recruitment: HaireX lifted hiring conversion 41%. - Restructuring was executed fast (EUR 109 million booked in H1; about 3,300 mainly European roles).
  2. Not improving. - Forecasting: the guide was cut nine months into the new chief executive's tenure. - Attrition stays at 66-68%. - Ethics-hotline reports doubled to 3,340 in 2025 from 1,643. - A former director says the sales team "frequently overpromises" relative to operations. - The Core Services margin fell 50 basis points to 10.5%, and the Americas core margin is 9.4%. - "Non-recurring" restructuring recurs every year.
C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

F2751st of 55

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The negative developments cluster around credibility and earnings quality, and they reinforce each other. A new team that cut its own first guide is likely to reset the mid-term targets and add restructuring, which in turn weakens the adjusted earnings basis on which the stock looks cheap.

Reasoning.

  1. Guidance credibility. FY2026 was cut within nine months; the Q2 "turn" was false; the mid-term targets will be revisited.
  2. Restructuring creep. EUR 70-90 million became EUR 120-140 million, at the high end. About EUR 100 million a year of "non-recurring" cash cost is now habitual: 15-18% of projected free cash flow against a peer median of 3-5%, in the 80th percentile of peer restructuring intensity. A new plan in February 2027 is plausible (my judgement).
  3. Insider selling in a cluster in August-September 2026 (C-11).
  4. Culture. A top-down legacy; operations polarised between Latin America and Europe; eight Nordic chief executives in ten years; country managers protecting local incentive plans; claims that internal groups drive positive Glassdoor reviews; ethics reports doubled.
  5. Execution profile. A consultant chief executive with no BPO profit-and-loss experience; sales overpromising relative to operations.
  6. Earnings quality (C-54).
  7. Specialized Services deceleration. -3.9% LFL in H1 and -2.6% or -2.9% in Q3, which lowers the value of the asset to be sold. Its 33% margin may not be a run rate.
  8. A possible goodwill impairment at FY2026 (C-09).
  9. A disposal that leaves a leveraged, deflating remainder (C-15).
C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

F2253rd of 55

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

Judgement. The culture is not holistic or congruent. It is polarised by region, rooted in headcount-volume incentives that conflict with outcome pricing, and shows strain in attrition, ethics reports and churn among regional leaders. The new team is trying to change it (regional employee boards, outcome-based incentives for managers), but goals, incentives and execution are not yet aligned.

Reasoning.

  1. Evidence of misalignment. - Under the founder, management was "aggressively top-down", "similar to a political boss" (expert). - Former directors describe merit-based transparency in Latin America and network-dependent bureaucracy in the UK and Europe. - Country managers protect local incentive plans rather than global client outcomes. - Morgan Stanley: a "major cultural overhaul" is needed to move away from pricing per full-time equivalent. - Attrition of 66-68%, against 45-55% at the best competitors. - Ethics reports doubled to 3,340 (2025), with 334 confirmed discrimination or harassment cases. - Claims that coordinated internal groups drive positive Glassdoor reviews. - An expert warns that the departure of legacy founders could "disrupt the company's people-first culture".
  2. Counterpoints. A high internal-promotion rate. Managers are now assessed on value captured from automated workflows rather than on headcount. Regional employee boards by end-2026.
  3. Contrast. Rational has 7.06% staff turnover and abolished its separate quality departments.
C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

D3538th of 55

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement. Four sudden risks matter most:

  • a top-ten client automating or insourcing a vertical in one move (moderately plausible; precedent exists);
  • a labour or reputational event in a delivery hub (low-moderate; precedent exists);
  • a US tax on offshored services (low; large);
  • the restructuring guide for FY2027 being set above EUR 140 million (moderate; it would invalidate the clean-earnings case).

A large goodwill impairment is moderately plausible and non-cash, but it would confirm the bear narrative.

Risks, with plausibility and size (sizes are my estimates unless stated).

  1. Top client automates or in-sources. Plausibility moderate. The largest client is 6% of revenue and the top ten 31%. Precedents: Trust & Safety, and Meta at TaskUs (-22%). Size: 3-6% of revenue on higher-margin seats, about 5-10% of EBITA.
  2. Labour or reputational event in a hub. Plausibility low-moderate. Precedent: November 2022, -34% in a day and a trading halt. Size: 20-35% on the share price; a risk to contracts with brand-sensitive clients.
  3. US offshoring tax (HIRE Act, 25%, non-deductible). Plausibility low. Size: concessions on US-facing offshore work (India about 6% of revenue, plus the Philippines and Latin America), perhaps 5-10% of EBITA.
  4. Philippine nationwide wage rise (PHP 100 a day). Plausibility moderate. Size: about 15% on the Metro Manila floor; with the peso at 25% of cost of goods sold, about 1-1.5 points of group margin if not recovered.
  5. FY2027 restructuring guided above EUR 140 million (the memo's invalidation line). Plausibility moderate, given the mid-term reset. Size: EPS on the clean basis down about 10%; the cheapness narrative breaks.
  6. Disposal fails, or is forced cheaply in a cracked credit market. Plausibility moderate. Size: removes the bull branch (the memo gives it 32% odds).
  7. Large goodwill impairment at FY2026. Plausibility moderate (C-09). Size: non-cash; perception.
  8. Outcome-based contracts miss their targets in an operation with high attrition. Plausibility low-moderate. Size: contract losses.
D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

D3837th of 55

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The drivers are concentrated, and concentrated in the wrong place for predictability. The difference between the forecast and the last four to six years is that revenue stops growing and EPS grows only below operating profit. Almost all of the EPS growth, about 98% of it to FY2030 and more than all of it in FY2027, comes from three lines: restructuring, interest and the share count. Two of those three are decisions of the board and are announced on single days. The operating lines, by contrast, are the net of large, diffuse and opposing flows across more than 1,500 clients and nearly 100 countries. They are steadier than they look, but they cannot be observed directly. The result is a forecast whose operating base is diffuse and stable and whose growth is concentrated and discretionary.

Reasoning.

  1. The revenue difference (from about +11% a year, and 8% organic in 2010-2019, to about zero) has one concentrated driver and one diffuse one. - Concentrated: Trust & Safety. A few hyperscale clients automated one vertical and took about 3 points off group growth in 2026. It is the reason the 2026 guide failed. It is now about EUR 360 million a year at the second-half rate, under 4% of revenue (my calculation), so the same clients cannot do the same damage again through this vertical. - Diffuse: net price including offshoring, about -2 to -2.5 points a year of Core revenue, spread over every renewal, against volume gains of about 2.5-3 points from vendor consolidation, back office and new work. Neither is disclosed. Diffuse drivers average out, which is why growth excluding Trust & Safety moved within a narrow band in 2026 (+1.7% to +2.0%) while the mix beneath it changed. - Client concentration sits between the two. The largest client is 6% of revenue and the top ten 31%. TaskUs shows what real concentration does (Meta went from 27% to 17% of its revenue in a year). Teleperformance's exposure to one client's decision is about a quarter of that.

  2. The margin difference is small because the gross flows cancel, not because nothing moves. In FY2027 savings add about 0.8 point and weaker delivery currencies up to 0.3-0.5 point; price, the loss of Trust & Safety margin, wages and the normalising of Specialized Services take away about 1.2-1.6 points (my estimates; D-01, point 3c). A flat margin is the difference between two numbers each several times larger than the forecast change. That makes the margin stable in normal conditions and exposed to any one flow moving by a third.

  3. The EPS difference is concentrated in three lines (my calculation on my changed inputs: clean EPS from about EUR 12.5 in FY2026 to about EUR 15.6 in FY2030, a rise of about EUR 3.15).

Source EUR per share Share of the growth Who decides it
Restructuring falling from EUR 140 million to 50 million about +1.1 35% Board and chief executive
Net financial items falling from EUR 240 million to 178 million about +0.75 24% Mechanical, given the cash flow
Share count (buybacks of EUR 500 million in FY2029-FY2030, net of vesting) about +1.3 40% Board
Recurring EBITA about +0.05 1-2% Clients, competitors, operations

On the model's unchanged inputs the picture is the same with a larger buyback share. In FY2019-FY2024, by contrast, EPS grew through revenue and acquired profit.

  1. For the next 18 months the concentration is extreme. FY2027 clean EPS rises by about EUR 0.62 on my inputs: +0.72 from restructuring falling by EUR 60 million, +0.24 from interest, -0.27 from recurring EBITA and -0.07 from the share count. On the second set's inputs it rises by EUR 0.95, of which restructuring and interest are 0.96. Either way, one line supplies three quarters or more of the gross growth, and that line was raised from EUR 70-90 million to EUR 120-140 million within 2026. Each EUR 10 million of restructuring is EUR 0.12 of clean EPS, about 0.9%; so is each 10 basis points of recurring margin. Adjusted EPS, which excludes restructuring, does not grow at all in FY2027 on my inputs (about EUR 14.0 against 14.1).

  2. What the concentration means for predictability. It cuts both ways. The few drivers that matter are easy to name and will be disclosed on known dates (the restructuring guide and the buyback stance on 2027-02-25). But a forecast that depends on one announcement is binary around that date, and the line in question is the one where the record is of envelopes raised. A new programme of EUR 120-140 million in FY2027 would remove all of the year's EPS growth; a guide of EUR 50 million (Morgan Stanley's assumption) would add about 3%. The operating base, being diffuse, will neither rescue nor sink the year. The board's choices will.

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

B+648th of 55

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The top of the company has changed almost completely in eight months, more than in the previous decade. The change signals that the board has accepted the founder model failed, and that it has moved to a strategist chief executive backed by an aligned reference shareholder, with a mandate on capital allocation and the portfolio. It does not yet signal operating renewal: the operator layer has thinned, and the first output under the new team was a guidance cut.

Reasoning.

  1. What changed. The founder Daniel Julien (in charge for nearly 50 years) stepped down on 2026-03-15 and stays as adviser for 18 months, to about September 2027. Jorge Amar became chief executive on 2026-03-16, after 14 years at McKinsey (leading its digital customer-care practice from 2023 to 2026) and nine months advising Teleperformance. Moulay Hafid Elalamy chairs the board; the chairman and chief executive roles are now separate. Deputy chief executive Thomas Mackenbrock resigned on 2026-03-15 and moved to the board. The finance director Olivier Rigaudy retired; Benoit Gabelle, deputy finance director since 2022 and before that a KPMG/EY transfer-pricing partner, was interim and then permanent from 2026-09-24. Andreas Braun became Chief AI Officer (May 2026). Juan Carlos Hincapie runs Specialized Services, succeeding Scott Klein. Trust & Safety has a new leader with client-side experience, investor relations a new head (Simon Zaks), and the board four co-opted directors, with Sheikha Hanadi Al-Thani as lead independent director. The board has 13 members and is 64% independent.
  2. Key actors. On 2026-03-02 the chairman entered a buy-side equity swap over up to 9,710,130 shares (up to EUR 355 million), taking Saham Group to 14.9%. FIL holds 10.1% of voting rights. Morgan Stanley reported swap positions above 5% in August 2026 and fell to 4.88% in September. My hypothesis, not a fact in the files, is that this is the hedge on the chairman's swap rather than an independent holder.
  3. What it signals. (a) A break with failure: the 2025 bonus paid zero points on financial criteria. (b) A mandate for a review of every segment and for cost reduction. (c) Owner alignment at chair level, against an insider median of 0.9% at Concentrix. (d) A designer of AI-native operations rather than an operator: the chief executive has no profit-and-loss experience in the industry, against a peer operator median of 11 years.
  4. Warning signals. Deputy chief executives keep leaving (Bhupender Singh in August 2024, Mackenbrock in March 2026 after seventeen months). Executives sold in a cluster in August-September 2026: Miranda Collard 4,323 shares on 2026-08-04; Agustin Grisanti 6,500 at USD 83.16 on 2026-09-14; Mackenbrock 14,500 at EUR 68.95 on 2026-09-17. And the new team's first guide, reconfirmed in July, was cut in November.

For the model. Credit the new team with decisiveness on cost and capital. Do not yet credit it with forecasting accuracy or revenue delivery.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

F5443rd of 55

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The strategy has changed materially and is clear on direction. Its economic bridge is not articulated, and its financial anchor (the 2028 targets) is now being withdrawn. People are well resourced; technology ownership and capital investment are thin. The choices are defensive and contain no lever that would make clients pay more.

Reasoning.

  1. What changed. The old line was Julien's: AI is for human augmentation, and growth comes from acquired scale. The new priorities are: - a hybrid human-and-AI model, with the company as "customer zero" of its own tools; - outcome- and transaction-based pricing (over 15% of revenue, growing mid-single-digit; outcome-based and back-office work grew double-digit on a two-quarter stack); - technology-agnostic partnerships (minority stakes of EUR 25.9 million in Sanas, Ema and Parloa; up to EUR 100 million budgeted for AI partnerships); - the TP.ai platform (over 1,400 projects; 35% of clients); - run-rate savings of EUR 150-170 million (up from EUR 100 million), costing EUR 120-140 million in 2026; - a portfolio review naming LanguageLine, TLScontact and Health Advocate; - deleveraging to 1.2 times by 2028; - no large acquisitions; - Trust & Safety renamed "AI safety"; - a Value Creation Office run with McKinsey.
  2. How well articulated. The direction is plain and well communicated. The transmission is not quantified: how much of the savings stays in the margin, what price deflation is assumed, and how outcome-based revenue reaches profit. The 4-6% organic growth and 15.5% margin targets for 2028 were reiterated in June and July 2026 and are now "to be re-visited" (Morgan Stanley, 2026-11-05). The strategy is losing its numerical anchor before it has produced one.
  3. How well resourced. People: yes (C-11). Capital: capital expenditure at 1.9% of revenue may be under-investment. Technology is bought through partners, and Bernstein calls the AI stakes "largely unnecessary with hindsight" because clients choose their own technology. The sales overhaul (AI-driven pricing tools) is resourced.
  4. The critique. The plan sells the one robust franchise and keeps the deflating core. For it to work the core must stop deflating, and nothing in the plan raises what clients pay. Outcome-based pricing moves delivery risk onto a workforce with 66-68% attrition; one expert calls this a "vicious P&L circle" when targets are missed.
C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

F1455th of 55

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Guidance has been lowered, not raised, and the mid-term targets are about to be lowered. The only figure raised (the savings target) measures the size of the problem. Expect a full reset with the FY2026 results (2027-02-25 in the FactSet block).

Reasoning.

  1. FY2026. LFL growth guidance was cut from 0-2% to -1.5% to -2.5% on 2026-11-05. Free cash flow was steered from EUR 1.1 billion (FY2024) to about EUR 800-850 million (adjusted). Restructuring was raised from EUR 70-90 million to EUR 120-140 million, now at the high end. The 14.6% recurring EBITA margin (at constant currency) was held.
  2. Mid-term (2028). The targets are 4-6% LFL growth, about 15.5% recurring EBITA margin, 1.2 times leverage and EUR 3 billion of cumulative free cash flow over 2026-2028; Morgan Stanley says they will be revisited. Consensus already disbelieves them. FY2028 revenue consensus is +0.8%. Cumulative FactSet free cash flow consensus for 2026-2028 is about EUR 2.47 billion (EUR 704 million, EUR 869 million, EUR 898 million) against the EUR 3 billion target. The definitions differ (FactSet does not deduct lease payments, the company's net free cash flow does), so the true gap on the company's definition is probably wider (my judgement).
  3. Record. Organic guidance has been cut in each of 2023 (pro forma target 10% to 6%), 2024 (to about 2%), 2025 (twice) and 2026.
  4. What a reset could do. A target low enough to be believed could become the "clearing" event that a corporate-change setup needs (see C-74). Four years of cuts argue that the market will discount any new target until it has been met once.

For the model. Use the FY2026 guide as the base year and do not model towards the 2028 targets. Treat the February 2027 framework as an event that is likely to lower the EBITA trajectory and possibly to add a new restructuring line.

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

F4540th of 55

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Simplification and exits at the edges are under way, but the material step has not been taken eight months after the review began. That step would not be an exit from a low-return unit: it would be the sale of the highest-return unit. Its value to shareholders depends entirely on the price and on how much of the proceeds goes to debt rather than to buybacks.

Reasoning.

  1. Done. The Russian subsidiary Direct Star was sold in December 2025, after a EUR 66 million impairment. Low-margin countries and unprofitable contracts are being exited (unquantified). Entities were simplified: an internal transfer of Teleperformance Portugal (EUR 700 million) and Majorel legal mergers. AllianceOne and TP Infinity USA moved from Specialized to Core Services Americas on 2026-01-01, the tidy-up a seller does before a sale.
  2. Under review. LanguageLine (about 75% of Specialized Services; about EUR 1,115 million of revenue in FY2025), TLScontact (about 10%) and Health Advocate. Bernstein: up to EUR 2.5 billion of proceeds (four businesses at about 6 times EBITA), EUR 1.8 billion returnable to shareholders (42% of today's market capitalisation). Morgan Stanley: sum-of-the-parts upside of up to 40%, though it expects the first sales to be small units (PSG Global, AllianceOne) and warns that selling high-margin assets dilutes the group margin. Nothing has been signed. Bernstein's note of 2026-11-09 is titled "Weaker top line and de-leverage focus".
  3. The arithmetic (memo, checked). Specialized Services earns about EUR 460 million of EBITA, about EUR 324 million after tax. Sold for EUR 2.5 billion, with EUR 1.8 billion spent on buybacks at about EUR 80 (about 22 million shares), the result is roughly EPS-neutral at a sale multiple of 5-8 times EBITA; it is clearly accretive only above about 8-10 times.
  4. The leverage after a sale (my rough calculation). If EUR 0.7 billion went to debt, the remaining core (EBITDA about EUR 1.4 billion) would carry about EUR 3.3 billion of net debt, about 2.3 times. That is higher than today, on a weaker business, and conflicts with the 1.2 times target. More of the proceeds may therefore go to debt than the bull case assumes.

For the model. Keep the disposal outside the base case. Model it as a scenario with three inputs: the sale multiple, the split of proceeds between buybacks and debt, and the margin of the remaining group (Core Services was 10.5% in H1 2026).

C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

F5341st of 55

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Entry into new markets is modest and mostly sensible, but small. Geographic moves add delivery capacity (cost arbitrage), not new demand markets. Product moves are close adjacencies that share customers and delivery skills. None is large enough to offset the deflation of the core in 2027-2028.

Reasoning.

  1. Geography, delivery side. India planned to reach 155,000 employees by 2026; South Africa; Egypt (about USD 0.80 an hour against an EGP 7,000 monthly minimum wage); buyout of the remaining 51% of the Qatar joint venture (EUR 8.8 million). These protect the margin but also deflate revenue.
  2. Products. - Data services for AI (labelling and annotation): double-digit growth, size undisclosed. - The TP.ai platform and agentic orchestration with Parloa and Ema (exclusive reseller rights). - TP Infinity consulting (700 consultants). - "AI safety", the repositioned Trust & Safety unit. - Outcome-based Sales and Collections work. - Agents Only, a crowdsourcing platform (USD 19.2 million). - Sanas accent neutralisation (USD 13 million stake).
  3. Is it sensible? In terms of distance from the core (the Wisdom Library's "Core versus adjacency growth"), these are close moves on shared customers and skills, which is the right kind. Two limits: a Concentrix director is sceptical of BPOs' long-term role in AI data services, and clients prefer to buy technology directly from software firms, so BPOs "struggle to monetise their R&D". The risk report's "False Friend #1" is the right caution: celebrating double-digit growth in small lines while the core shrinks.
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

F1355th of 55

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The company is not raising prices in real terms; prices in the core are falling in nominal terms. Price recovery is latent only in Specialized Services and in a small outcome-based niche, and is not yet evidenced in reported revenue. Management talks about capturing value through outcome-based pricing; it does not talk about price increases.

Reasoning.

  1. Falling. - Renewal discounts of 15-25%, against single-digit historical adjustments. - "Automation discounts" of 5-10% on hourly labour, because clients expect agent productivity gains to be passed back. - Offshoring lowers billing rates by 20-30%. - Renewals occur "at lower absolute spend". - Management conceded in Q&A that "pricing pressure is real".
  2. Partial nominal indexation. Cost-of-living clauses in some Latin American contracts (Latin American operations +7% in local currency). They do not recover statutory costs (Colombia's 42-hour week, night surcharges, the 23% minimum-wage rise).
  3. Latent. The Specialized Services margin rose 330 basis points to 33.0%; one expert attributes part of it to temporary price negotiation, and part comes from moving LanguageLine work onto Core Services' offshore delivery. One IT expert says BPOs keep "stable pricing and command premiums" for agentic AI solutions, and another BPO expert claims margins above 60% under outcome-based pricing for agentic resolution. Both are single, low-confidence sources.
  4. Analogue. Rational put through a 4.9% price increase with no customer pushback. That is what real pricing power looks like, and Teleperformance has nothing comparable.

For the model. Model a negative net price/mix effect of about -1 to -2 points a year in Core Services (my judgement, from the evidence above and the offshoring drag), offset partly by productivity. Hold Specialized pricing flat.

C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

C+6014th of 55

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Acquisitions have been stepped down sharply, and that is the right decision. The record it replaces is poor: large deals bought at full prices into the most disruptable part of the industry, synergies below target, and repeated impairments. The one recent bolt-on that worked (ZP Better Together) was in Specialized Services.

Reasoning.

  1. Record. Acquisitions took 61% of capital spent since FY2020 (Morgan Stanley). Intelenet (2018): USD 1.045 billion at 12 times EBITDA before synergies. Majorel (November 2023): EUR 2.58 billion at 8 times pro forma EV/EBITDA before synergies. Synergies were EUR 94 million in 2024 and EUR 20-30 million more in 2025, against a EUR 150 million target for end-2025 (Deutsche Bank): about 75-85% delivered (my calculation). Integration brought "significant operational and cultural challenges". Critics call Majorel a deal that raised exposure to disruptable voice work. Impairments since: EUR 97 million (PSG, Health Advocate) and EUR 66 million (Russia).
  2. The exception. ZP Better Together (February 2025, USD 500.9 million, funded by debt) contributed USD 188 million of revenue and USD 65 million of operating profit in FY2025, a margin of about 35%.
  3. Now. Only small items: AI minority stakes (EUR 25.9 million), Agents Only (USD 19.2 million), the Qatar joint venture (EUR 8.8 million). FactSet shows EUR 2 million of acquisitions in H1 2026.
  4. Judgement. The discipline is partly forced (leverage, a share price at five times earnings) and partly chosen (the new chief executive). The score credits the absence of further value-destroying deals, not a capability to integrate.
C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

F4240th of 55

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The balance sheet is being used less, not more. Returns are flat: the dividend has been held and buybacks have stopped. Management is mooting deleveraging, not buybacks. That is prudent given the Fragility tilt and an equity that is about 55% of enterprise value. It costs value per share if the earnings are durable, and the deleveraging path creates buyback capacity from late 2027 even without a disposal.

Reasoning.

  1. Dividend. EUR 4.50 for FY2025 (+7.1% from EUR 4.20): about 53% of reported EPS and about 45% on the Viewforth definition. EUR 262 million was paid in H1 2026. Consensus: EUR 4.55 (FY2026), EUR 4.63, EUR 4.80. The yield is about 6.3-6.4% at EUR 71.3.
  2. Buybacks. EUR 184 million in 2024 and EUR 114 million in 2025 (about 1.3 million shares, inside a EUR 150 million programme); zero in 2026. Deutsche Bank says management is "holding off on share buybacks to prioritise debt reduction", and investors want a timeline. The stated policy of returning "up to two-thirds of net free cash flow" (2025) is not being applied.
  3. Leverage path. 2.0 times at end-2025 and 2.1 times at June 2026 on the company's definition. Bernstein forecasts 1.7 times at end-2026 and 1.2 times at end-2027, meeting the 2028 target a year early. That gives room to buy back from late 2027 or 2028 (my inference). Free cash flow is about 70% weighted to the second half, so the 1.7 times for 2026 depends on H2 cash.
  4. Economics. At a price-to-earnings ratio of 4.8 times, the earnings yield is about 20%, against an after-tax cost of debt of perhaps 3-4%. A buyback beats debt repayment if the earnings hold. Deleveraging is right if the earnings are wrongly measured (C-54) or deflating (C-01). The board's choice says it fears the second.
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–62

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Several positive forces are mechanical and well founded: the lower tax rate, the phasing of savings into 2027, deleveraging, the Trust & Safety base effect, and structurally low capital intensity. One is a genuine signal: the chairman's capital commitment. Two depend on how contracts are priced: the flexibility that high attrition gives, and internal AI productivity.

Reasoning.

  1. The reference shareholder. The chairman's EUR 355 million swap was taken at the point of maximum pessimism. His record includes selling Saham's insurance business to Sanlam for USD 1.1 billion at 25 times (2018). He is a dealmaker in the chair, which raises the plausibility of a disposal.
  2. Tax. The effective rate fell from 33.1% to 29.0% (H1 2026) and is guided below 30% for FY2026: about +5% to EPS, already partly in Deutsche Bank's +8% EPS revision.
  3. Savings phasing. About half of the EUR 150-170 million run-rate is expected in 2026. The other half (about EUR 75-85 million) arrives in 2027: about 75-85 basis points of revenue if retained (my calculation).
  4. Deleveraging. Interest savings, and buyback capacity from late 2027 (C-19).
  5. The base effect. Trust & Safety's drag shrinks from 2027 (C-01).
  6. Flexibility from attrition. With 66-68% attrition, headcount can be reduced by slowing recruitment, without severance (Bernstein). This is a natural hedge against volume loss.
  7. Internal AI productivity. 20-30% faster call resolution, 20-40% less rework, and half of quality-assurance work automated. This helps margin only where pricing is not per hour.
  8. Capital intensity. Working from home and capital expenditure near 2% of sales keep cash conversion high.
  9. Commercial. The H1 2026 sales overhaul (double-digit bookings growth; a large US bank win ramping).
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

B+646th of 55

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The one controlling actor, the chairman and his Saham Group, has signalled large scope for change, by action rather than by statement. The action points to the portfolio and capital allocation rather than to a new commercial model. There is no activist, and the voting structure protects the incumbents.

Reasoning.

  1. Saham and Elalamy (14.9% through the swap; chairman). He co-founded Majorel, which Teleperformance bought in 2023, so he sold the business to the company he now chairs. He brought Saham's chief executive, Moulay Mhamed Elalamy, onto the board in 2026. He separated the roles, chose Amar, mandated a review of all assets, and committed up to EUR 355 million. The scope signalled for portfolio action is high. A swap is economic exposure that can be unwound, so its unwinding would be a sentiment risk (C-82).
  2. Other holders. FIL holds 10.1% of voting rights and is passive. Morgan Stanley's position is probably a hedge (C-11). There is no activist at Teleperformance, while Impactive has invested USD 266.7 million in Concentrix.
  3. Structure. Double voting rights after four years (double the French peer norm), but low voting asymmetry (2.45%). The founder waived 33,335 performance shares and his influence ends with his advisory term in September 2027.
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

F5446th of 55

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The positive changes are strong in people and intent, moderately well resourced, and narrow in their outputs. They are concentrated in cost and capital (savings, tax, deleveraging, the possible disposal) rather than in the revenue engine. Four or five of the twelve internal forces are strong (chief executive, other leaders, strategy, the owner's commitment, cost standards). The ones that would drive earnings are weak or negative: guidance down, price falling, new markets small. The new team's first measurable output was a guidance cut. The self-help is real but runs on a treadmill: the savings are needed just to hold the margin (Reader's key, item 3).

C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

F2253rd of 55

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Judgement. The staircase is thin and back-loaded. Its first dated step (the FY2026 results and framework on 2027-02-25) is more likely a reset than an upgrade. The only high-novelty upward step, a Specialized Services disposal, has no date and weakening foundations. The well-founded steps (the Trust & Safety base effect, deleveraging into buyback capacity) are mechanical, of low novelty and already partly expected.

Next 6 months (to May 2027).

  1. 2027-02-25 (FactSet date; the memo said 2027-02-18): FY2026 results, the FY2027 guide, a likely reset of the 2028 targets, possibly a new restructuring plan, and the impairment test. High novelty: the first full framework under Amar. Its occurrence is well founded; its direction is uncertain. Positive only if the new targets are believable and restructuring is then guided down (below EUR 50 million would meet the memo's re-entry condition).
  2. Conclusion of the portfolio review. No date. High novelty. Moderate-to-low foundation: eight months with nothing signed; small units are more likely first; the latest stance is "de-leverage focus".
  3. End-2026. Regional employee boards; the former finance director's advisory role ends (2026-12-31). Low novelty.
  4. Concentrix's Q4 print (around January 2027). A perception event.
  5. Q1 2027 revenue (late April 2027). The first quarter lapping the steep Trust & Safety declines. Moderate novelty; well-founded arithmetic.
  6. The May 2027 annual meeting. The dividend (consensus EUR 4.55) and the buyback authorisation.

Months 6 to 18 (May 2027 to May 2028).

  1. H1 2027 results (late July 2027). The first test of savings retention: a recurring margin of 15% or more would move the memo's savings view to probable. Moderate novelty.
  2. Leverage near 1.2 times by end-2027 (Bernstein), so buybacks could resume. High novelty for this stock. Moderate foundation.
  3. Disposal completion and capital return, if signed.
  4. The founder's advisory term ends (September 2027). Low novelty.
  5. India's data-protection law is fully enforced (2027-05-13). Spring 2027: the French presidential election, a perception event.
  6. FY2027 results (February 2028).

Months 18 to 36 (May 2028 to late 2029).

  1. The 2028 targets fall due, or their reset version. The first vesting of the long-term plan (free cash flow over EUR 2.3 billion).
  2. Organic growth turns positive as Trust & Safety becomes immaterial (about 3% of revenue). Moderate novelty. Its foundation depends on Core Services price deflation.
  3. The outcome-based share reaches the 25-30% that the earnings review set as the test by Q4 2027.

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

F2754th of 55

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

F2754th of 55

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Teleperformance is the largest company in an industry whose structure protects incumbents against one another but not against the substitute that now sets the price. Consolidation helps share, not price. The profit pool is migrating upstream to software and AI owners and into regulated niches (LanguageLine). Competitors defend volume with discounts, and new technology-first entrants carry no legacy headcount. The historical pattern of outperformance (growth from outsourcing and offshoring penetration, with stable margins) is absent, and every element of the underperformance pattern is present. Steer for the model. Volume share gains of about a point a year from consolidation, net price/mix in Core Services of about -1 to -2 points a year, and no structural pricing recovery before 2028. Specialized Services is the only part of the value chain where Teleperformance holds a defensible profit pool.

C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

F4050th of 55

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The route to market is direct enterprise sales, which the company has just strengthened. The intermediaries that now matter, consultancies, IT integrators and software platforms, increasingly design the customer-experience architecture and keep its profit pool, which leaves the BPO as the execution layer chosen afterwards. That is a moderate and rising hindrance.

Reasoning.

  1. Helping. The H1 2026 sales overhaul (AI-driven pricing tools; double-digit bookings growth). TP Infinity's 700 consultants, to get upstream. Exclusive reseller rights with Parloa and Ema. ISG named the company a leader in AI-powered delivery on Amazon Connect.
  2. Hindering. Clients "prefer to buy software separately", which leaves BPOs "unable to replace lost seat revenues with high-margin software licences". Enterprise software vendors earn gross margins near 80% against Teleperformance's 22%. Systems integrators embed agentic platforms. Procurement-led tenders favour price.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

D4235th of 55

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Competitors' behaviour hurts on price. Teleperformance's position against its labour-based peers is resilient on margin, cash and leverage. That resilience rests partly on Specialized Services: on Core Services alone, Teleperformance is already the lower-margin of the two leaders. A sale of Specialized Services would make it the weaker of the two.

Reasoning.

  1. Relative strength. Group recurring EBITA margin about 14.5% against Concentrix's 12.3-12.8% in FY2025 and 12.6% non-GAAP in Q3 2026. Free cash flow to sales 9.5% over four years against 7%. Leverage 2.1 times against 2.5 times.
  2. The insight. Teleperformance's Core Services margin was 10.5% in H1 2026, below Concentrix's group margin. The bases differ (segment recurring EBITA against group non-GAAP operating margin), but the direction is clear.
  3. Behaviour (C-28, C-29): discounting in rebids; selectivity at Concentrix; volume-chasing at Foundever and Telus; TaskUs capturing technology-first accounts.
C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?F3845th of 55full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

F1455th of 55

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Disruption acts on every actor, and in every case the result works against Teleperformance. It is a price-taker on both sides: towards clients, who capture productivity, and towards technology suppliers, who capture the profit pool and pass on compute costs. The position is fragile.

Reasoning.

  1. Demand (clients). They automate and in-source, mandate their own tools, and reprice at renewal. Strongly negative.
  2. Competition. Technology-first entrants and IT services gain the AI-era share. Negative.
  3. Regulators. AI transparency slows disruption (mildly positive); labour laws raise costs (negative).
  4. Suppliers (AI and cloud platforms). They sell direct to enterprises, raise token prices, and leave no reseller margin (Deutsche Bank: Teleperformance "cannot extract meaningful technology re-seller fees"). Negative.
  5. Labour. Attrition erodes the skills needed to hit outcome-based targets. Negative.
