Group judgementGroup judgement: Industry and Value Chain (Layer 1)F2754th of 55full answer under 3 Value chain dynamics and industry structure
C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

C-6426th of 55

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Teleperformance is the joint number one in outsourced customer experience, with about 9-10% of the global market, level with Concentrix. In Specialized Services, LanguageLine is described as the dominant US interpretation provider. Leadership by size is secure. Leadership in the work clients are now buying (contracts won since AI arrived) is not proven.

Reasoning.

  1. Shares. Bernstein puts both leaders at about 9%; Morgan Stanley gives Teleperformance 10% and Concentrix 9%. Next come Foundever (4%) and Telus Digital (2%). Teleperformance's share rose from about 4% in 2014, mostly by acquisition (Intelenet, Majorel).
  2. Size. Revenue EUR 10,209 million (FY2025) against Concentrix's USD 9,826 million. About 490,000 employees against 455,000. Nearly 100 countries and 400 languages.
  3. The caveat. Concentrix says 50% of its Q3 2026 revenue came from contracts won and deployed since AI arrived. Teleperformance gives no comparable figure, so its standing in AI-era work is unmeasured.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

F2551st of 55

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The market is fragmented. The top five hold about 20-27%, below the 30% test. The distribution is steep at the very top (two leaders at 9-10% each, roughly three times the next two combined), then a long tail of regional players and, above all, in-house captive operations. Prices are therefore set by the tail and by the in-house alternative, not by the leaders, so concentration gives no pricing power.

Reasoning.

  1. Figures. The business-foundations report puts the top five at 20%; the memo's sum of the named shares is about 27%. The two leaders together hold about 19%, against about 6% for numbers three and four (Foundever 4%, Telus 2%). No figure for numbers six to ten is given, but they are below 2% each.
  2. The real competitor set. 60-65% of customer-care capacity is still in-house. The relevant concentration is that of the whole customer-care spend, where the leaders hold about 3%.
  3. Conduct. Sub-scale and regional operators "chase volume with little focus on profitability".
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

F3549th of 55

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. At the top, shares have been stable to rising, but only through acquisition. On organic growth Teleperformance is losing ground to technology-led and IT-services competitors. New entrants have not taken a measurable share of the base, but they are taking a growing share of the new work, and that is where instability is rising.

Reasoning.

  1. Organic comparison, 2026. Teleperformance -1.2% LFL in Q2 and Q3. Capgemini +5.1% pro forma organic in Q2. Cognizant +4.1% at constant currency in Q2. TaskUs +19% in FY2025 and +13.2% year on year in one period (its business outside the top client grew 15% in Q2 2026); its digital customer-experience segment grew 7.1% in Q3 2026. Concentrix about +1-2% organic in H1 and -1.2% reported in Q3. Telus Digital -7% (FY2025).
  2. New entrants. "High-end consulting and IT service firms increasingly capture market share in complex technological customer-experience transformations." AI-native operators and software vendors billing on outcomes win specific deployments. No quantified share exists in the files.
  3. Why it matters for the model. Structural share loss of perhaps 1-2 points of organic growth a year to technology-first competitors (my judgement) is offset by consolidation gains against regional players (C-08).
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

F4541st of 55

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Share consolidates towards the largest: client vendor panels are shrinking and regional players are failing. Whether it consolidates towards the most advantaged is a different question, and the most advantaged in the next cycle may be the technology-first players. Teleperformance is among the largest; it is not shown to be among the most advantaged. It is gaining share of a revenue pool whose value per unit is falling.

Reasoning.

  1. Towards the largest. Morgan Stanley: enterprises are "actively streamlining vendor rosters to concentrate volumes with scale leaders". Experts: scaled BPOs will dominate because they can combine labour and technology. Atento is losing clients.
  2. Towards the most advantaged. A former Concentrix director calls Teleperformance industry-leading in scale but "perceived as less innovative and too rooted in legacy call-center models". Its bundles of technology with labour have failed to sell. Concentrix (the iX suite, Live Voice Translation in over 60 languages, an agentic migration engine on Microsoft Marketplace) and TaskUs show more product depth.
  3. The Wisdom Library's "A flywheel is not a moat" and "Learning and adaptation is a race, not a moat". Scale in labour does not compound in an industry where the technology layer is bought from outside.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

F2453rd of 55

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. At the commodity end the attitude is volume first, and prices do fall when volume falls. The leaders claim margin discipline but discount in rebids. The industry is not benign.

Reasoning.

  1. Sub-scale peers chase volume "with minimal regard for contract profitability" (Concentrix management, via Morgan Stanley). Regional competitors "reduce overall contact center pricing discipline".
  2. Concentrix cut non-complex work from 7% to 5% of revenue in 2025 and says it bids only for complex, margin-accretive contracts, yet it is "increasingly offering upfront discounts and concessions" in rebids.
  3. When client volumes fall, clients demand "the same service levels with fewer full-time equivalents", and providers shift "from pricing to win growth to pricing to defend and retain existing logos". So prices fall when volume falls.
  4. Losses are tolerated at the distressed end (Atento). Mid-sized regional players "face severe pressure to survive without the capital to invest in technology".
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

F2454th of 55

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The attitude has deteriorated over the last 18-36 months, from pricing to grow to pricing to defend, and it is becoming less stable. A "shark attack" is plausible from two directions: a wounded Concentrix defending volume, and technology-first operators who carry no legacy headcount and can price per outcome below Teleperformance's cost.

Reasoning.

  1. The change. Deals with upfront discounting rose from 10-15% (2025) to 15-25% (mid-2026). Discounts at renewal of 15-25% compare with single-digit adjustments historically.
  2. Reactive behaviour. Peers' capacity overhang from tariff-related pauses in client projects pushed their margins 80 basis points below expectations, the kind of situational pressure that triggers discounting.
  3. Shark-attack candidates. Concentrix: two impairments totalling about USD 2.6 billion, an activist, revenue falling. AI-native operators: USD 0.50 against USD 2 per transaction in one client's example. For them, cutting price is a business model, not an attack.
  4. The Wisdom Library's "Money war and last man standing" and "Competitive intensity is entry minus exit". Entry is getting cheaper (technology) while exit is slow (labour-heavy incumbents with leases and contracts). That combination predicts falling prices.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

F2754th of 55

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The structure is weakly supportive and the support is fragile. What supports it (consolidation, compliance and scale barriers, switching costs, outsourcing penetration of only 32%) protects Teleperformance against other labour-based BPOs. It does not protect it against the in-house and AI substitutes that now set the price. As the substitute improves, the support weakens, and each renewal cycle (now about annual) tests it.

C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?F2055th of 55full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

F1555th of 55

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Substitution (clients' self-service and in-house AI, and consumer agents) is the central threat and is strengthening. Its limits are in complex, empathetic, regulated and brand-sensitive work, which slow it but leave the simple volume exposed.

Reasoning. 20-50% of standard volumes are seen as automatable over a few years, and 15-20% within 12-24 months (Bernstein, Morgan Stanley). Trust & Safety has already been substituted at speed. Voice, 64% of interactions, is the next frontier (Jefferies). Limits: Klarna's reversal, the human-in-the-loop return, and transparency under the EU AI Act. The Wisdom Library's "Demand purification" applies: clients accepted the compromise of a human seat because nothing better existed; AI removes that compromise for simple tasks.

Memo topic 3, : the Rater's score

4a

Business quality

F2454th of 558 answers
Judgement24C-4130C-4258C-4330C-4435C-4512C-4630C-4712
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

F2454th of 55

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Teleperformance's advantages are size, switching costs and compliance, which keep other labour-based BPOs out. Its one durable moat (LanguageLine's accuracy mandates) is the asset it proposes to sell. It has no pricing power in Core Services, shares scale economies only because clients force it to, and runs on a treadmill where each year's effort holds the margin flat. Steer for the model. Hold the competitive position as stable in volume share. Erode Core Services pricing by about 1-2 points a year. Do not credit any moat-driven margin expansion. If the disposal happens, re-base the remaining group to Core Services economics: about 10.5% recurring EBITA margin in H1 2026, against 14.5% for the group.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

F3054th of 55

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. In Core Services the company holds barriers against new labour-based entrants rather than advantages over the substitutes that now matter, and they are narrowing. The one strong, durable moat is regulatory and accuracy-based, in LanguageLine. The Wisdom Library's distinction applies: "barriers block entrants, advantages beat rivals". Teleperformance has barriers.

By moat type.

  1. Customer-related (switching costs): moderate, narrowing. A two-month retraining ramp for a new provider. Six-month GDPR and HIPAA audits before a switch. Embedding in clients' back-end systems. The top 100 clients have stayed 14 years on average. Narrowing because contracts are moving to annual renewal, and because AI agents codify workflows, which lowers switching costs. The business-foundations report names a fall in that 14-year tenure as its leading warning signal.
  2. Production-related (delivery footprint): moderate, eroding. Nearly 100 countries, 400 languages, India heading to 155,000 staff. Deutsche Bank: the historical delivery advantages "are gradually eroding" as peers copy them.
  3. Scale: moderate; stable against small BPOs, weak against technology-first competitors. Procurement terms with AI vendors, the capacity to fund technology programmes, survival in vendor-panel cuts.
  4. Regulatory and political: strong in Specialized Services, stable to slightly narrowing; moderate in Core Services. LanguageLine serves healthcare and legal settings where interpretation must be accurate. Its margin of 30-35% reflects this. AI translation and US immigration policy are the threats. In Core Services, compliance certification (ISO 27701, DORA, the EU AI Act) favours scale.
  5. Culture and knowledge: weak. A top-down legacy, 66-68% attrition, and a reputation as "less innovative".
C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

C5819th of 55

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Teleperformance is the largest by revenue and headcount, about 4-5 times the size of the number three. That scale confers survival in vendor consolidation, compliance credibility, global coverage and the capacity to absorb restructuring. Scale in labour is also a liability: legacy headcount and an incentive culture built on headcount counter-position the company against automation.

Reasoning.

  1. Size. EUR 10.2 billion of revenue against Concentrix's USD 9.8 billion; Foundever about 4% share; Telus Digital USD 2,777 million. 490,000 employees against 455,000.
  2. What it confers. Preferred-partner status in panel cuts; the ability to fund EUR 120-140 million of restructuring in a year; purchasing terms with AI vendors; 400-language coverage.
  3. What it does not confer. Pricing power, technology ownership or an AI-era cost advantage. Morgan Stanley says moving away from pricing per full-time equivalent needs a "major cultural overhaul because the group has historically been incentivised by headcount volume". Wisdom Library: "Counter-positioning is a delay, not a moat" and "Scale needs a bounded market". The market in labour hours is not bounded; it is being substituted.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

F3555th of 55

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Against peer BPOs, Teleperformance is demonstrably not inferior. Against the alternative that matters most for simple work, client-built and AI self-service, the utility of a human seat is falling and is already inferior for routine Tier-1 tasks.

Reasoning.

  1. Evidence it is not inferior to peers. - It wins in vendor consolidation (Morgan Stanley). - Client relationships last 14 years. - A Concentrix director calls it "a clear leader in trust and safety", and it signed a major contract expansion with TikTok. - Bookings grew double-digit after the sales overhaul; a large US bank win was ramping. - ISG recognised it as a leader on Amazon Connect. - Its margins and cash conversion are above Concentrix's.
  2. Evidence against. "Perceived as less innovative". Mid-sized clients report missed service levels because of attrition. A Teleperformance former director says sales pitches "clash with operational realities".
  3. Against the alternatives. Clients expect automated systems to cut processing costs to USD 0.50 per invoice from USD 2. For complex, empathetic and regulated work, humans keep the edge (Klarna; Gartner, 54%).
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

D3038th of 55

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Sharing happens de facto and under duress, not by choice. Clients extract the productivity gains at renewal ("productivity-sharing commitments"), and outcome-based pricing presents this as a partnership. The difference matters: a moat built on shared scale economies (the Costco model) needs the company to be the lowest-cost provider and to give savings away first in order to win share. Here the savings are taken from it, and its cost position against AI substitutes is not the lowest. The Sector digest's judgement that the largest outsourcers can use AI savings "to bid below smaller rivals while holding margins" may apply in tenders against small BPOs. It does not show up yet in Teleperformance's organic growth.

C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

F1254th of 55

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. The mechanical forces work against the company in each period. AI deflation, wage inflation in the hubs, attrition and shorter contracts erode its position steadily. Only heroic effort, a new restructuring programme each year and perhaps a disposal, holds the margin. This is a treadmill, not a flywheel (Wisdom Library: "Flywheel vs Treadmill"; "Red Queen effect").

Reasoning.

  1. Against, every period. Price give-back at each renewal. Statutory and wage cost increases in Colombia and the Philippines. Compute cost. Contract terms shortening. A technology layer improving outside the company.
  2. For, every period. Vendor consolidation. A rising compliance bar. Distress among small rivals. Offshoring's effect on margin. From 2027, the Trust & Safety base effect (a one-off, not a recurring force).
  3. Evidence. EUR 150-170 million of savings is needed to keep the margin flat at 14.6% (Reader's key, item 3).

C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

F4551st of 55

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. The strategy points the right way and is sensible: hybrid delivery, outcome pricing, neutrality on technology, cost reduction and portfolio review are what every broker asked for. It is not great. It does not widen a moat, it does not make growth the priority, and its boldest element, selling Specialized Services, makes the remaining group less resilient across environments. It is dynamic on cost (attrition gives flexibility) and static on the revenue model.

Reasoning.

  1. Right. The diagnosis (seat-based pricing is dying) is correct, and outcome pricing is the logical answer. Partnering rather than building avoids betting on one technology.
  2. Missing. A mechanism to keep productivity gains (clients take them); proprietary technology clients will pay for; a growth engine of size.
  3. Fit across environments. - Slow AI adoption: the plan works (cost out, cash returned). - Fast AI adoption: outcome pricing transfers risk to a high-attrition operation, and a smaller, Core-only group is more exposed. - Recession: vendor consolidation helps, but volume and price both fall.
  4. Wisdom Library, "Diagnose the decline before choosing the strategy". The decline is in the price of the core. Selling the healthy part treats the balance sheet, not the decline.

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

Judgement. The company is moving from management-first to shareholders-first, driven by an aligned chairman and the board's refresh. The historical record of capital allocation (empire-building acquisitions) and some governance features (auditor tenure, double voting rights, related-party approvals, a low hurdle in the long-term plan) still weigh. Evidence for shareholders first.

  1. The chairman's EUR 355 million commitment.
  2. The dividend rose from EUR 2.40 (2019) to EUR 4.50 (2025).
  3. Buybacks in 2024-2025.
  4. The long-term plan is over 60% of chief-executive pay value.
  5. A fraud-activated clawback with a two-year look-back, and a rule to keep 30% of vested shares.
  6. The founder waived 33,335 performance shares.
  7. Pay followed performance: zero financial-criteria points for 2025, and the founder's variable pay was USD 1.05 million (40% of target).
  8. Chairman and chief executive roles separated; the board 64% independent.
  9. Prudent scaffolding: early refinancing, twelve-month currency hedging, a leverage target.

Evidence against.

  1. Acquisitions took 61% of capital since FY2020, at high multiples, into voice work.
  2. Related-party transactions with Bhupender Singh and Thomas Mackenbrock were approved at the May 2025 meeting.
  3. The audit committee is 67% independent. Deloitte has been auditor since 1999 (the leadership report) or 2011 (the risk report); the reports disagree.
  4. Double voting rights after four years.
  5. The board can waive the chief executive's non-compete at its discretion.
  6. The long-term plan's free-cash-flow hurdle is "over EUR 2.3 billion", against a public target of EUR 3 billion.
  7. Executives sold in a cluster.
  8. Pay is set on adjusted earnings that exclude recurring restructuring.

C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

F3050th of 55

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. The scope for high-return reinvestment inside the business is small. The highest-return use of cash is buying back the company's own shares at about 20% earnings yield, and that return is only real if the earnings hold. Mid- and long-term potential is therefore flat to low growth plus capital return. That supports a fair multiple in the mid-single digits on adjusted earnings until organic growth stabilises, not a return to the five- or ten-year averages.

Reasoning.

  1. Internal reinvestment. AI data services (double-digit, small); outcome-based contracts, which need upfront transformation spending ("providers are increasingly asked to fund upfront capacity and training"); India capacity; TP.ai. None is sized, and clients resist buying BPO technology.
  2. Acquisitions. Off the table, and with a poor return record (C-18).
  3. Buybacks. At 4.8 times the 24-month-forward price-to-earnings ratio the earnings yield is about 21%, against an after-tax cost of debt of about 3-4%.
  4. Fair multiple. Today's 24-month-forward multiple is 4.79 times. The two-year median is 4.55 times and the five-year median 5.87 times. Ten-year bands run from 8.0 times (10th percentile) to 16.4 times (median). For a business with organic growth near zero, a 6% dividend yield, falling estimates and leverage of about 2 times, I judge the fair multiple at about 5-7 times adjusted earnings until two quarters of positive organic growth are reported (my judgement). The upper end needs the earnings basis to be trusted (C-54).

F2043rd of 55

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. The earnings base is not clean. Adjusted EPS exceeds reported EPS by more than half. The largest adjustment that recurs in substance, restructuring, is excluded every year. The cheapness of the shares (4.8 times) depends on the market continuing to accept the adjusted basis.

Reasoning.

  1. The gap. FY2025 brokers' adjusted EPS of EUR 13.20 against reported basic EPS of EUR 8.48: 56% higher. Adjusted net profit exceeded reported by EUR 284 million in 2025, driven by EUR 218 million of amortisation of acquired intangibles, plus EUR 97 million of impairments. The adjustments were about 25% of reported profit, against an industry median of 15-18%, and about 35% including impairments.
  2. Recurring "one-offs". Restructuring was EUR 109 million in H1 2026 and EUR 120-140 million for FY2026, and has averaged about EUR 100 million a year. Deutsche Bank: "labelling hundreds of millions in recurring severance as one-offs unsustainably inflates adjusted EPS."
  3. Capitalised costs and financing. EUR 326 million of capitalised software; EUR 110 million of receivables factored.
  4. Tax leakage. The reported tax rate was 36.8% in FY2025 and 39.8% in FY2024, against an adjusted rate of about 29-33%. The H1 2026 rate of 29.0% is genuine progress.
  5. A clean figure (my calculation). Treating about EUR 130 million of restructuring as recurring (about EUR 92 million after tax, about EUR 1.55 a share), and keeping acquired amortisation excluded, gives FY2026 EPS of about EUR 11.9 against consensus EUR 13.46. The price-to-earnings ratio on that is about 6.0 times, not 4.8 times.

For the model. Carry two EPS lines: brokers' adjusted, and "clean" with restructuring at a run-rate (EUR 80-130 million a year until shown otherwise).

C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

D4537th of 55

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Leverage is moderate on solvency measures and comfortable on maturities. Its effect on the equity is large: debt is about 45% of enterprise value, so small changes in enterprise value or interest rates move the equity and EPS disproportionately. Deleveraging is a modest tailwind to EPS from 2027.

Reasoning.

  1. Levels. Net debt was EUR 4,013 million at June 2026 (company, 2.1 times; FactSet including leases, 2.29 times). Net interest was EUR 282 million in FY2025 (FactSet, including leases): about 23% of FactSet adjusted EBIT, interest cover about 4.3 times. Average maturity is 4.5 years after the EUR 1.2 billion bond of May 2026. Covenants are not at issue.
  2. EPS sensitivity (my calculation). A 100 basis point change in rates on about EUR 5 billion of gross debt (to the extent it is floating or refinanced) is about EUR 50 million before tax and EUR 35 million after: about 4% of EPS.
  3. Equity sensitivity (my calculation). Enterprise value is EUR 7,631 million and market capitalisation EUR 4,251 million. A 10% fall in enterprise value is about an 18% fall in the equity, and one turn of EV/EBITDA is about 45% of the market capitalisation.
  4. Deleveraging. If net debt falls to about EUR 2.7-3.0 billion by end-2028 (EUR 2.5 billion of free cash flow less about EUR 0.8 billion of dividends, with no buybacks; my estimate), the interest saving is about EUR 50-70 million before tax: about +4-6% to EPS.
  5. Environment. Fragility is "Double Underweight" in the Fitness for Fighting table. The market penalises leverage within groups of defensive stocks.

5a

Pricing power

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

F1551st of 55

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Price will subtract from revenue in every year of the forecast, by about 2 to 2.5 points a year of Core Services revenue once offshoring deflation is included, and by about 0 to 1 point in Specialized Services. Volume (vendor consolidation, back office, outcome-based work, data services) adds back about 2.5-3 points, which is why Core growth excluding Trust & Safety settles near +1%, not near zero. The price drag on margin is about 50-80 basis points a year before productivity; the savings programmes are what neutralise it. The price effect is likely to worsen before it improves: the share of deals with upfront discounting rose in mid-2026 and contracts now renew about annually, so the 2026 discounting reaches revenue through 2027. There is no evidence of pricing power anywhere in the business except, in a weakened form, at LanguageLine.

Reasoning.

  1. The components of net price in Core Services (each from the AlphaSense pricing report unless stated). - Productivity cannibalisation: historically 1-1.5% a year, about 2% now, rising to 3-4% as generative AI scales (Nuvama). - Renewal and rebid pressure: 3-10% a year on mature rebids (channel check, 2026-05-07); renewals flat to 5% lower at Concentrix (channel check, 2026-11-04); technology and financial renewals contracting 8-12% a year where Concentrix concedes price to keep scope (2026-05-12). Total-contract-value discounts of 5% rising to 12% once productivity commitments are included (2026-11-04). - Upfront discounting: deals with upfront discounts rose from 10-15% (2025) to 15-25% (mid-2026); bid discounts of 8-15% to win transformation deals. - Offshoring: India billing rates about 70% below US onshore (USD 6-10 against USD 22-35 an hour); a drag of about 150 basis points a year on Teleperformance's organic growth (Morgan Stanley); Concentrix guided to about 3 points of drag going into 2026. - Partial indexation: cost-of-living clauses in Latin America (local-currency growth of 7% in Brazil), which recover wage inflation but not statutory costs; global clients "rarely recognise" inflation automatically. My synthesis: net price including offshoring of about -2 to -2.5 points a year in Core, of which about 1.5 points is offshoring (margin-accretive) and about 0.5-1 point is like-for-like price (margin-dilutive).

  2. Why price falls most when volume falls. Concentrix's filing says its pricing is "characterised by flat unit prices", so in-flight contracts do not reprice; the repricing comes at renewal, and renewals are now about annual. When client volumes fall, buyers ask for the same service with fewer full-time equivalents. The 2026 data point (Trust & Safety automated, a guidance cut) is therefore followed by a 2027 repricing round, not by relief.

  3. Outcome-based pricing does not yet hold price better. It is over 15% of revenue, growing mid-single-digit, with outcome-based and back-office work growing double-digit on a two-quarter stack in Q3 2026. Management calls it structurally accretive, but discloses no margin. Concentrix says outcome contracts "have not gained the momentum" hoped for; Firstsource has about 50% of revenue outcome-based and guides 50-75 basis points of annual margin expansion; Genpact keeps transaction pricing a niche. A Foundever paper (2026-08-19) says outcome pricing cuts cost per contact by 15-25% for the client: the client's gain is the vendor's lower revenue per unit. At Teleperformance's current mix, a doubling of the outcome share would not change the group's price trajectory before 2028.
  4. Specialized Services has a weaker form of pricing power, and it is defended by cost, not price. LanguageLine (about 65-75% of the segment) is protected by legal mandates for human interpreters in US healthcare and legal settings; it continued to gain share into Q3 2026 (Bernstein). But management "adjusted the cost per minute of LanguageLine to a lower level" (Q2 2025 call), and the margin rise to 33.0% came from cost control, the internal synergy plan (Core's offshore delivery) and easy comparisons, not price. AMN Language Services and Propio compete on price through apps; Bernstein (2026-02-23) expects the segment to shrink low-to-mid single-digit as AI translation pressures price per transaction. TLScontact has no pricing power at tender (the UK loss cost about 60 basis points of group margin in FY2025; VFS Global holds about 65-70% of the market against TLScontact's about 8%).
  5. Revenue contribution, year by year (my estimates, points of group LFL growth).
FY2026e FY2027e FY2028e FY2029-30e
Net price incl. offshoring, Core (weighted to group) -1.8 -2.1 -2.1 -2.0
Price, Specialized (weighted) -0.1 -0.1 -0.1 -0.1
Volume and new work, Core excl. Trust & Safety +3.4 +3.0 +3.5 +3.4
Volume, Specialized (weighted) -0.3 0.0 +0.1 +0.1
Trust & Safety -3.1 -1.4 -0.6 -0.4
Group LFL about -1.9 about -0.6 about +0.8 about +1.0

The Trust & Safety line is volume, not price, but I show it because it is the swing item in the arithmetic.

  1. Margin contribution. Like-for-like price give-back of 0.5-1 point of Core revenue is EUR 40-85 million a year of gross margin, about 40-85 basis points of the group; offshoring adds back margin (its labour cost falls faster than its price). Net, before savings, about -50 to -80 basis points a year. This is the main reason I hold the recurring margin flat despite the savings (P-03).
  2. The cone and its skew. Downside: Nuvama's 3-4% cannibalisation arrives by 2028, or consumer agents such as Meta's Muse begin to remove volume as well as price, taking Core excluding Trust & Safety to about -1% and group LFL to about -1% in 2028. Upside: the pass-through stabilises at about 2%, vendor consolidation adds a point of share, and group LFL reaches about +2% by 2028. The downside is more likely than the upside because every recent data point (discount share, contract length, renewal discounts) has moved against the company.

For the model. Group LFL of -1.9%, -0.6%, +0.8%, +1.0%, +1.0% (FY2026-FY2030); currency of about -0.6 points (FY2026) and about +0.6 points (FY2027), then zero. No price-led margin expansion in any year.

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7535P-0135
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

F3547th of 55

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Operating leverage to volume is low, because labour is mostly variable and attrition lets headcount flex. Operating leverage to price is high, because a price cut that is not matched by productivity falls straight to profit. The range of outcomes is therefore set by pricing at renewal, not by volume.

Reasoning.

  1. Cost structure. Personnel is 65-70% of Core Services revenue and follows contracted volume; attrition of 66-68% allows quick adjustment. Sites (the second-largest cost after salaries), IT and overheads are semi-fixed.
  2. Volume (my estimate). The contribution margin on lost or gained volume is about 25-30%. A 1% change in volume (about EUR 100 million of revenue) moves recurring EBITA (about EUR 1.46 billion) by about EUR 25-30 million: about 1.7-2.0 times leverage.
  3. Price (my calculation). A 1% price reduction not matched by productivity takes about EUR 100 million off EBITA: about 7% of EBITA.
  4. History. Margins stayed within about ±100 basis points through 2009 (organic -1.1%) and 2020, and from 15.0% (FY2024) to 14.6% (FY2026 guide) while organic growth fell from about +2% to about -2%. Cost programmes absorbed it. The Core Services margin, though, fell from 12.4% to 10.5%.
  5. Range for 2027-2028 (my judgement). Base case 14.0-15.2% recurring EBITA margin. Bear case: Deutsche Bank's 12.7% for FY2028, and Morgan Stanley's 11% terminal, if price deflation outruns the savings.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

F3547th of 55

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Fixed-cost operating leverage will barely shape the line items over the next four years, because revenue will barely move and the cost base is mostly variable. The leverage that does shape them is leverage to price: a point of price given back and not matched by productivity costs about 7% of recurring EBITA, ten times what a point of volume costs. Over 2021-2026 the same structure produced a group margin held at about 14.4-15.1% while organic growth swung from +25.7% to about -2%, because cost programmes and the higher-margin Specialized Services mix absorbed the price loss. For 2026-2030 I expect the same treadmill: recurring EBITA of EUR 1.43-1.46 billion, flat in money terms and 5-7% below the FY2024 level of about EUR 1.54 billion, with no operating leverage to lift it in either direction.

Reasoning.

  1. The cost structure makes volume leverage low. Personnel is about 70% of Core Services revenue (EUR 6,157 million against EUR 8,724 million in FY2025, the 2025 Universal Registration Document). Attrition of about 67% a year (5.6% a month) means headcount follows volume within weeks. Semi-fixed costs (sites, IT, overheads; external expenses of EUR 605 million in H1 2026, about 12% of revenue) are the only source of fixed-cost leverage, and 35-40% of agents work from home. My contribution margin on volume is about 25-30% (the first set's estimate, which the precedents support), so a 1% change in volume (about EUR 100 million of revenue) moves recurring EBITA by about EUR 25-30 million, about 2% of EBITA and about 2.5-3% of clean EPS.

  2. The precedents show low fixed-cost leverage, not the "severe de-leverage" the AlphaSense report asserts. I disagree with the report's headline reading of its own evidence: - Teleperformance 2021: LFL growth of +25.7% lifted the recurring EBITA margin by 230 basis points (12.8% to 15.1%), about 9 basis points per point of growth. - Concentrix 2021: revenue +18.4%, non-GAAP operating margin +230 basis points (10.8% to 13.1%). - TTEC 2009: revenue -16.6% and operating income -7.5%, so its margin rose (about 7.8% to 8.6%). The report cites this as evidence of rigid costs; the arithmetic shows the opposite. - Teleperformance 2023-2026: organic growth fell from about +2% to about -2%, and the group margin moved only from 15.0% (FY2024 pro forma) to 14.4% (FY2026, my forecast, reported currency). - Teleperformance 2009: revenue +3.5% and the margin fell 170 basis points (10.2% to 8.5%), in a smaller, more onshore company. This is the one precedent of material de-leverage on flat revenue, and it came with price and reorganisation costs. - Atento 2021-2022 is the exception that proves the rule: a collapse of volume from its main client together with currency devaluation, which no labour model can flex fast enough. The empirical rule is about 10-15 basis points of group margin per point of organic growth, before management action.

  3. Price, not volume, is where the leverage sits. A 1% net price cut that productivity does not offset removes about EUR 100 million of EBITA (about 7% of recurring EBITA, about 9.5% of clean EPS). The evidence on price is negative (P-04): renewal give-backs, automation discounts and offshoring (about -150 basis points a year of organic growth). The Future Forward savings (EUR 150-170 million run-rate, 1.5-1.7% of revenue) are about the size of one year's price and wage pressure. That is why management held the FY2026 margin flat at 14.6% while raising the savings target (Deutsche Bank, 2026-07-31).

  4. The group margin hides a falling core, and the mix that props it up is finite. Core Services' recurring EBITA margin fell from 12.4% (FY2024) to 11.9% (FY2025, restated) and 10.5% (H1 2026, -50 basis points). Specialized Services rose to 33.0% (H1 2026; 31.0% in FY2025 restated). Specialized is about 14% of revenue but about 31% of recurring EBITA (about EUR 440-450 million of about EUR 1,430 million). Morgan Stanley estimates that 90 basis points of the group's 250 basis point margin gain from 2016 to 2024 came from mix. That support is now neutral or negative: Specialized revenue is falling (-3.9% LFL in H1 2026, about -2.6% to -2.9% in Q3), part of its margin gain came from moving LanguageLine work onto Core's offshore delivery (a one-off), and the business is for sale.
  5. What this means for each line, against the last four to six years (my base case; detail in P-10).
Line FY2021 FY2024 FY2025 FY2026e FY2027e FY2028e FY2030e
Revenue (EUR m) 7,115 10,280 10,209 9,951 9,951 10,031 10,233
Recurring EBITA margin (company) 15.1% 15.0% pro forma 14.5% 14.4% 14.5% 14.5% 14.3%
Recurring EBITA (EUR m) about 1,074 about 1,540 about 1,480 1,433 1,443 1,455 1,463
EBITDA margin, FactSet operating basis (after restructuring) 19.4% 18.9% 19.0% 17.8% 18.4% 18.5% 18.4%

The EBITDA line on the model's basis falls more than recurring EBITA in FY2026 because of EUR 140 million of restructuring, and recovers in FY2027 mainly because restructuring falls to EUR 80 million, not because the business improves. The next Analyst should not read the FY2027 margin gain as operating leverage.

  1. The core's margin path, segment by segment (my estimates). Core Services: about 11.5% (FY2026), 11.7% (FY2027), 11.8-11.9% (FY2028), helped by the retained third of the savings, the end of the high-margin Trust & Safety drag and offshoring mix, and held back by price. Specialized: about 32% (FY2026), 31.5% (FY2027), 31% (FY2028), as the internal synergy gain is lapped and LanguageLine's price per minute softens. I judge the core estimate to carry the most risk: a further 50 basis point loss in Core (one more year like H1 2026) would take the group to about 14.0%.
  2. Skew and cone. For FY2027 the recurring margin range I can defend is 13.5% (bear: LFL -3%, a 2-3 point price give-back in Core) to 15.0% (bull: LFL +1.5%, savings retained). The skew is to the downside: the bear case needs only a continuation of the 2026 pattern; the bull case needs the first year of retained savings since 2021. Deutsche Bank's 12.8% for FY2028 is the credible tail; Bernstein (14.6% for FY2028) and Redburn (14.5% flat to FY2028) bracket my base case.

For the model. Do not model a margin that responds to revenue. Model the margin as the net of four inputs: net price/mix in Core (-1 to -2 points a year), gross savings (EUR 75-85 million more in 2027, then a new programme of about the same size every two to three years), mix (neutral to slightly negative), and hub wage and currency effects (net small positive in 2027 as the peso and rupee weaken and twelve-month hedges roll off).

Financial leverage

P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

C5618th of 55

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Financial leverage will add modestly to EPS through falling interest from 2027, and more strongly through buybacks from 2028, but only if the operating earnings hold. On my cash flows deleveraging is slower than the sell-side assumes: about 1.8 times at end-2026 and about 1.5 times at end-2027 on the company's definition, against Bernstein's 1.7 times and 1.2 times. The 1.2 times target is therefore a 2028 event, not a 2027 one, and buybacks are more likely to restart in 2028 than in late 2027. The leverage matters more for the equity's risk than for its EPS: with net debt at about 45-49% of a lease-inclusive enterprise value, every 10% fall in enterprise value takes about 20% off the market value, and every 5% miss in EBITDA at a constant multiple takes about 9-10% off it.

Reasoning.

  1. Where the balance sheet stands. Net debt of EUR 4,013 million at June 2026 on the company's definition (2.1 times), EUR 4,020 million on FactSet's (2.29 times on its trailing EBITDA). Both include leases of about EUR 720 million. Financial net debt excluding leases is about EUR 3.3 billion (debt of EUR 4,324 million less cash of EUR 1,027 million). Average maturity is 4.5 years after the EUR 1.2 billion bond of May 2026, issued at about 5.0%, which lifted the average cost of debt to 4.28% from 3.85% (company, H1 2026 slides). Covenants are not at issue.
  2. Net financial items (including about EUR 45 million of lease interest). EUR 282 million in FY2025 (FactSet). I forecast EUR 240 million (FY2026), 220 million (FY2027), 205 million (FY2028), 195 million (FY2029) and 185 million (FY2030). Deutsche Bank's financial result is EUR 231 million and 194 million for FY2026-FY2027; Redburn's net financial interest (which I read as excluding lease interest) is EUR 163 million and 138 million. The fall is about EUR 55 million over four years, worth about EUR 39 million after tax, about +5% of net income. It is slowed by refinancing older debt at about 5% (each EUR 1 billion refinanced costs about EUR 10-12 million more a year) and by buybacks from 2028.
  3. The deleveraging path on my cash flows.
End of FY2025 FY2026e FY2027e FY2028e FY2029e FY2030e
Net debt incl. leases (EUR m) 3,892 about 3,440 about 2,980 about 2,660 about 2,430 about 2,230
Net debt / EBITDA before restructuring 2.0x 1.8x 1.5x 1.4x 1.25x 1.15x
Without buybacks 1.3x 1.05x 0.8x

The walk assumes net free cash flow of EUR 734-796 million a year, dividends of EUR 262-290 million, EUR 25-40 million a year of small acquisitions, and a stable lease liability. Bernstein's faster path needs free cash flow near EUR 913 million in FY2027, which in turn needs restructuring to stop (P-03).

  1. Buybacks are the larger EPS lever, and they are back-loaded. The board's stated priority is debt; it has bought nothing in 2026 (EUR 184 million in 2024, EUR 114 million in 2025). The stated policy of returning up to two-thirds of net free cash flow is not being applied. I model EUR 150 million (FY2028), 250 million (FY2029) and 300 million (FY2030), at assumed prices of EUR 75, 80 and 85. That reduces the year-end share count from about 58.7 million (FY2027) to about 51.0 million (FY2030), about -13%, and adds about +2%, +5% and +10% to EPS in FY2028-FY2030 against a no-buyback path, net of lost interest. At today's price (EUR 71.3; 4.8 times 24-month-forward earnings) each EUR 100 million retires about 1.4 million shares (about 2.4%), net of lost interest about +2% to EPS.
  2. The balance sheet's real weakness is the equity base, not the debt. Market value (EUR 4,251 million) is below FactSet's FY2026 book equity (EUR 4,384 million), which is mostly goodwill. Under IAS 36 that is a trigger for the FY2026 goodwill test, reported on 2027-02-25. Concentrix booked USD 1.52 billion (FY2025) and USD 1.05 billion (Q3 2026) for the same reason. The company has disclosed thin headroom only in Spain (goodwill EUR 99.8 million; a EUR 12 million impairment under a combined 100 basis point discount-rate and 50 basis point margin stress). An impairment would be non-cash, excluded from adjusted EPS, and would not affect covenants; its effect would be on the narrative and on reported EPS (P-08).
  3. How leverage amplifies the equity. On a lease-inclusive enterprise value of about EUR 8.35 billion, net debt including leases is about 48%. A 5% shortfall in EBITDA (about EUR 95 million) at a constant 4.3 times EV/EBITDA removes about EUR 410 million of enterprise value, about 9.6% of the market value. Rates: a 100 basis point rise on the floating and refinanced part of about EUR 4.3 billion of gross financial debt would cost up to EUR 43 million before tax, about 4% of net income; my reading is that most debt is fixed-rate bonds, so the near-term effect is a fraction of that.
  4. Peer read-across. Concentrix carries about 2.4 times (Bernstein, FY2026) to 2.7 times (Redburn) and is also prioritising debt; TaskUs about 1.4-1.5 times; Pernod Ricard at 3.7 times suspended its buyback and moved to a scrip dividend. Teleperformance is the least leveraged of the large customer-experience groups after TaskUs, and its leverage is a client-facing strength in vendor consolidation (Bernstein, 2026-11-09). It is not a leverage problem; it is an equity that is a leveraged slice of a low-multiple enterprise.

For the model. No buybacks in FY2026-FY2027; EUR 150 million, 250 million and 300 million in FY2028-FY2030. Net financial items as above. Treat the disposal as a scenario (P-08) and the goodwill test as a reported-EPS and narrative event, not a cash event.

5d

Cost-out scope NTM

2 answers
C-7752P-0338
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

C5219th of 55

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. About EUR 150-170 million (about 1.5-1.7% of revenue, about 11% of recurring EBITA) is being cut, and a further EUR 50-100 million is plausible in a new plan (my estimate). Cutting is not the constraint; keeping the savings is. Most of the cost base is agent labour, and under per-hour billing its reduction passes to the client.

By cost line (my judgement where the files are silent).

  1. Agent labour (65-70% of Core Services revenue). Reducible through AI productivity, offshoring and attrition. Margin-reducible only within outcome- and transaction-based contracts (over 15% of revenue) or by lowering attrition costs.
  2. Recruitment and training. Meaningfully reducible: HaireX (+41% conversion), training cut to two weeks, USD 80-100 per replacement agent.
  3. Real estate. Partly reducible: 35-40% work from home, room to consolidate further.
  4. Overheads (SG&A). The core of the current programme: the Value Creation Office, about 3,300 mainly European roles, voluntary departures in France, Majorel duplication.
  5. IT and technology. Rising (compute and token costs). Not cuttable.
  6. Low-margin countries and contracts. Being exited. This cuts cost and revenue together.

Retention. Management's flat margin guide for 2026 implies almost no net retention this year. The memo assumes about one third retained by 2028.

P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

F3846th of 55

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Of the EUR 150-170 million of Future Forward run-rate savings, I expect almost none to reach the recurring margin in FY2026, about EUR 25-30 million (about 25-30 basis points) in FY2027 when the second half of the savings arrives, and nothing further after that, because clients take most of the productivity at renewal and hub wages and compute costs rise. The largest effect on the reported line items comes instead from restructuring charges falling from EUR 140 million in FY2026 to EUR 80 million in FY2027 and about EUR 50-60 million a year later. That fall, not retained savings, lifts clean EPS by about EUR 0.7 a share in FY2027. The scope to cut is not the constraint; the scope to keep the saving is.

Reasoning.

  1. The programme and its phasing. Future Forward was raised to EUR 150-170 million of run-rate savings from EUR 100 million (company, 2026-07-30). About half is to be realised in H2 2026 and the rest in 2027. Restructuring cost is EUR 120-140 million for FY2026, at the high end; EUR 109 million was booked in H1 2026, of which EUR 92 million was personnel expense, for about 5,400 employees in Europe, the Middle East and Asia-Pacific, including about 3,300 mainly European roles and EUR 56 million of French reorganisation cost. The payback on cost is about one year in name.
  2. Why FY2026 retains almost nothing. The 14.6% margin guide (constant currency) was held while the savings target rose; Deutsche Bank calculates the uprating alone was worth about EUR 30 million in 2026 and asks why it does not lift the guide. Morgan Stanley's answer (2026-07-30) is that lower Trust & Safety volumes absorbed it. My arithmetic: the H2 2026 recurring margin implied by 14.4% reported for the year is about 15.2%, against about 15.4% in H2 2025. The savings are holding the margin against price, mix and wages; they are not adding to it.
  3. What reaches FY2027. The second half of the run-rate (about EUR 75-85 million) arrives in 2027. Retention evidence: renewal pass-through of productivity has historically been 10-25% (an expert call, which AlphaSense rates low in confidence) and is rising under agentic AI; the sector rule is that clients claim most automation savings at renewal (Sector digest, 2026-10-22). I assume one third is retained, about EUR 25-30 million. Against it: Specialized normalising (about -30 basis points on the group), Trust & Safety mix (about -10 basis points), the second Metro Manila step (PHP 25 a day from January 2027) and Colombian statutory costs, partly offset by weaker delivery currencies once hedges roll off (about +10 to +20 basis points). Net: recurring EBITA margin +10 basis points, from 14.4% to 14.5%.
  4. What reaches 2028-2030. Nothing incremental from this programme. The treadmill (P-05) requires a new programme every two to three years to hold the margin. I model restructuring at EUR 60 million (FY2028) and EUR 50 million a year after that, a lower run-rate than the four-year average of about EUR 100 million, because the large European overhead cuts are done. A new programme announced with the reset on 2027-02-25 is the main risk to that: a EUR 100-150 million plan would cost about EUR 1.2-1.8 of clean EPS spread over 2027-2028.
  5. Which cost lines are cuttable (the evidence, condensed). - Agent labour (about 70% of Core revenue): reducible through AI productivity, offshoring and attrition, but margin-reducible only under the over-15% of revenue that is outcome- or transaction-based. A former Teleperformance vice-president says the company prefers worked-hour pricing because outcome contracts carry penalty risk; that protects the margin from penalties but also means the productivity goes to the client. - Recruitment and training: genuinely reducible. HaireX raised hires per recruiter 31% and conversion 41%. Each agent replaced costs USD 80-100 (expert). - Real estate: partly reducible; leases constrain the pace and client consent is required for home working. - European overhead (about 3,300 roles): being cut now, with EUR 56 million of French cost; the liabilities were not all recognised in H1 2026 because local negotiations continued, so some cash follows in 2027. - Compute and hosting (Azure, AWS, model providers): rising, not cuttable; Morgan Stanley says still a small fraction of BPO costs. - Exits from low-margin countries and contracts: cut cost and revenue together; small.
  6. Peer precedents for retention. Pernod Ricard's EUR 900 million programme raised its operating margin 64 basis points while its gross margin fell 216 basis points. Concentrix delivered its Webhelp synergies (USD 95 million run-rate in Q4 2024, USD 120 million targeted for FY2025) and its margin still fell. TaskUs is guiding its adjusted EBITDA margin down to 19.0% (2026) from 21.1% to fund AI investment. No precedent in the files shows a labour-based customer-experience provider expanding its margin from a cost programme during flat or falling revenue.
  7. Timing for monitoring. The first test is the H1 2027 recurring margin (late July 2027). Against 13.6% in H1 2026, I expect about 13.9%; 14.2% or better would show that more than a third of the savings is being retained; 13.6% or below would show none is.

For the model. Recurring EBITA margin 14.4% (FY2026), 14.5% (FY2027-FY2028), 14.4% (FY2029), 14.3% (FY2030). Restructuring inside EBITDA at EUR 140 million, 80 million, 60 million, 50 million and 50 million.

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4712P-0535
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?F1254th of 55full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

F3546th of 55

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Three loops will dominate, one for each part of the income statement. Revenue: the Trust & Safety drag is a balancing loop in arithmetic (its weight shrinks, so its drag falls from about 3 points of group growth in 2026 to about 1.4 points in 2027 and about 0.5 points by 2028), but the automation that caused it is a slow reinforcing loop now spreading into voice, technical support and collections, at a rate of about 2-4% of revenue a year. That spread, not Trust & Safety, decides 2028 organic growth, and it caps it near +1%. Margin: the savings treadmill is a balancing loop that holds the recurring margin at about 14.3-14.5% and keeps restructuring recurring. EPS: the deleveraging-to-buyback loop is the only strong positive reinforcing loop, and it starts working in 2028. The impairment loop is a reinforcing negative loop on perception, with its next test on 2027-02-25. No loop in the evidence lifts revenue or margin structurally.

The loops, one by one.

  1. Trust & Safety automation: self-limiting in its own vertical, spreading outside it. - Mechanism: a few large technology clients automate moderation; the vertical shrinks fast; as its weight falls, its drag on group growth falls even if the decline rate holds. - Evidence: 8.0% of revenue (FY2025) to 5.5% (nine months of 2026), half of the November guidance cut (Redburn, 2026-11-06); about -30% organic in Q4 2026 (Bernstein, 2026-11-09). TaskUs's Trust & Safety revenue fell 12% in Q2 2026 as Meta automated (Morgan Stanley, 2026-08-11). - Spread: a Concentrix AI bot automated 40% of a client's transactions in its first month and cut that contract's revenue 12% (Q3 2024 call); doValue's Coeo resolves 70% of standard German collections cases without a person (DNB Carnegie, 2026-09-25); Concentrix reports a 65% advisor-productivity gain at a technology client (2026-09-29); an Everise executive predicts a 70% fall in human-managed volume (expert, 2025-12-15). Counter-evidence: Klarna's re-hiring, the return of "human in the loop", and the EU AI Act's disclosure rule slow it; Bernstein (2025-09-12) argues current AI lacks the emotional intelligence for complex voice work. - My judgement: the spread is real but works through renewals and per-contract productivity, so it shows up as a continuous 2-4% a year of revenue cannibalisation (Nuvama's figures) rather than as a second Trust & Safety-style step. A second step in one vertical (a top-ten client automating a programme) remains a 15-25% probability event in any 18-month window (my estimate), worth 3-5% of revenue. - Effect on the line items: group LFL improves by about 1.3 points from 2026 to 2027 mechanically, and by about 1.4 points more by 2028; underlying cannibalisation takes back about 0.5-1 point of that improvement.
  2. The savings treadmill: balancing on margin, reinforcing on the earnings gap. - Mechanism: price given back at renewal; a programme cuts cost to hold the margin; the programme costs restructuring; the next renewal round gives the saving back; a new programme follows. - Evidence: savings raised to EUR 150-170 million while the margin guide stayed at 14.6%; restructuring raised from EUR 70-90 million to EUR 120-140 million; charges of about EUR 128 million (FY2023), EUR 112-119 million (FY2024), about EUR 30 million (FY2025) and EUR 140 million (FY2026e). Deutsche Bank calls the programmes "structural characteristics of BPOs". Bernstein's counter (2026-11-05) is that free cash flow of EUR 800-850 million (adjusted) shows the programmes are accretive. - Effect: holds the recurring margin at 14.3-14.5% through 2030 in my base case; keeps restructuring at EUR 50-80 million a year after 2026; keeps the adjusted-to-clean EPS gap at EUR 0.6-1.9 a share.
  3. Offshoring: reinforcing on margin percentage, balancing on revenue. - Mechanism: clients move work to India, the Philippines, Egypt, South Africa; billing rates fall 20-70%; wages fall more (up to 90%); the margin percentage rises; the extra margin funds concessions that win more offshore work. - Evidence: offshore and nearshore delivery approaching 60% of revenue (2025 proxy); about 150 basis points a year of organic drag (Morgan Stanley); Concentrix's offshore shift raised its margin percentage while compressing revenue, with one to two quarters of duplicate costs. - Limits: data-sovereignty floors in healthcare and public services; about 15% of Concentrix's onshore revenue is movable; Philippine wage rises (12.2% in Metro Manila, staggered to January 2027) and peso exposure (25% of cost of goods sold). - Effect: about -1.5 points a year of revenue growth; about +10 to +20 basis points a year of margin, which is part of what offsets price.
  4. Vendor consolidation: reinforcing on share, too weak to beat deflation. - Evidence for: clients cutting panels in favour of scale and balance-sheet strength (Morgan Stanley, Bernstein 2026-11-09); one bank moving from three vendors to two (expert, 2026-05-29); Morgan Stanley's base case lifts Teleperformance's share from 7.7% (2023) to 8.4% (2028). - Evidence against: Nuvama (2026-10-19) finds AI uncertainty pushing clients to test several vendors on one function; Concentrix wins share and still shrinks. - Effect: about +0.5 to +1 point a year of volume, already inside my Core volume line (P-04).
  5. The impairment loop: reinforcing on perception, neutral on adjusted EPS and cash. - Mechanism: market value below book equity, a quantitative goodwill test, a write-down, a "value destroyed" headline, a lower multiple, a renewed trigger. - Evidence: market value EUR 4,251 million against FactSet FY2026 book equity of EUR 4,384 million; Concentrix's two write-downs were "necessitated by" its low market value; Teleperformance's EUR 97 million (FY2025, PSG and Health Advocate). - Effect: a plausible charge at FY2026 (reported 2027-02-25). The disclosed headroom is thin only in Spain; the group-level test could still produce a larger charge if auditors reject a control premium. It would lower reported EPS and book equity, not cash.
  6. Deleveraging to buybacks: the one positive reinforcing loop on EPS. - Mechanism: free cash flow cuts debt; at 1.2-1.5 times the board restarts buybacks; at 5-6 times earnings each EUR 100 million retires about 2% of the shares; EPS rises; more cash per share follows. - Evidence: the stated policy (up to two-thirds of net free cash flow), Bernstein's buyback yields of 2.7% (2026) and 7.1% (2027) if the policy were applied, and the board's current choice of debt first. - Effect on my numbers: starts in 2028; worth about +2%, +5% and +10% to EPS in FY2028-FY2030 (P-02). It is fragile: it needs free cash flow near EUR 770-800 million, and it would be cut first if a second Trust & Safety-style step hit revenue.
  7. Attrition and outcome contracts: a plausible negative loop that the company currently avoids by not taking the risk. Attrition of 67% (FY2025), below the COPC benchmark of 87% that the company cites but above the 45-55% of the best peers; an expert describes a "vicious P&L circle" of missed outcome targets. A former vice-president says Teleperformance prefers worked-hour pricing. The loop therefore caps the upside (the company cannot keep productivity it does not take risk on) more than it threatens the downside.
  8. TP.ai and AI data services: no evidence yet of a client-win loop. Over 1,400 projects (up about 80% a year) at more than 35% of clients, and double-digit growth in data services from an undisclosed base. Concentrix bundled its own AI platform into 40% of new wins and has over USD 60 million of platform revenue; Teleperformance discloses no revenue figure. Group growth excluding Trust & Safety of +1.7% (H1 2026) and +2.0% (Q3 2026) is the ceiling of what the loop has delivered so far.

How they combine on the line items.

Line Dominant loop, 2027-2028 Direction Effect in my base case
Organic revenue Trust & Safety base effect (balancing) against cannibalisation spread (reinforcing) Improving rate of change, then a ceiling -1.9% to -0.6% to +0.8%
Recurring margin Savings treadmill (balancing) Flat 14.4-14.5%
Restructuring and clean EPS Treadmill (reinforcing the earnings gap) Recurring EUR 50-80 million a year after 2026
EPS from 2028 Deleveraging to buybacks (reinforcing, positive) Up About +2% to +10% a year of accretion
Reported EPS and book equity Impairment loop (reinforcing, negative) Event risk at each annual test Not in the base case

For the model. Model the Trust & Safety drag explicitly (-3.1, -1.4, -0.6, -0.4 points); keep Core excluding Trust & Safety near +1%; carry a step-loss scenario of 3-5% of revenue at a 25-30% contribution margin (5-10% of EBITA).

To what extent is company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

C-71

How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

F2051st of 55

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement. The cut FY2026 guide is realistic, mildly conservative at the top of the range. The mid-term (2028) targets are not conservative at all: 4-6% organic growth against a last-two-year run rate of about +1% to -2%, under input forces that are worse than in the past. Adjusted for those forces the targets are about five points of growth too high, which is why a reset is expected.

Reasoning.

  1. FY2026. The top of the range (-1.5%) implies Q4 at about Q3's -1.2%. The bottom (-2.5%) implies about -5.5% (my calculation). Given the worsening that management itself acknowledged, the midpoint (about -3.5% for Q4) is a central case, not a conservative one.
  2. The 14.6% margin target. Supported by half of the savings landing in 2026. Mildly conservative given the 20 basis point beat in H1, if Q4 volumes do not fall further.
  3. 2028 against the past. The 2010-2019 average was 8.0%, the 2025 rate 1.0%, the 2026 rate about -2%. The external forces (C-01 to C-10) are worse than in any period on record, so a mean-reversion argument does not apply.

Case simplicity

7 answers
