Group judgement

Stock market narrative

F2253rd of 55

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The market narrative has no strong positive novelty lined up for the next six months. The dated event (2027-02-25) is most likely a reset of the targets. The undated one (the disposal) carries the whole bull case but has neither a price nor a clock. The well-founded improvements (the base effect, lower leverage, buyback capacity) arrive in late 2027 and 2028 and are not novel. The narrative therefore depends on the February reset being low enough to be believed. Until then, perception risk (AI-agent launches, Concentrix, France) outweighs positive news flow. Steer for set 2. The 18-month forecast's predictability rests on two quarterly series: Core Services organic growth excluding Trust & Safety, and the recurring margin at H1 2027. Revenue news is likely to be negative or neutral before mid-2027.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–68

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The two earlier sets are highly congruent in their conclusions and less congruent in several of the figures on which the conclusions rest. Both describe the same company: a no-growth, cash-rich labour business whose price per unit of work is falling, whose margin is held by cost programmes, and whose EPS growth comes from below operating profit. No answer in either set contradicts that spine, and the second set's numbers follow from the first set's steers. The incongruities are of three kinds. First, seven points of fact or arithmetic that I correct below; the most important are the timing of the Trust & Safety base effect, the half-year shape and margin of FY2027, and the date of the buybacks. Second, two gaps between the second set's cross-check and the model, both mechanical, neither showing a wrong input. Third, one large incongruity between everything in the two sets and the multiple at which the model values the shares. The corrections all point the same way, towards a slightly weaker and later path, so they lower the level of the forecast a little without changing its character.

Reasoning.

  1. The spine is the same in both sets, and every group of answers supports it. - External forces (C-01 to C-10, scores 15-40) say that price per unit of work is falling and that no industry driver accelerates demand. The second set's price answer (P-04, score 14) puts it at about -2 to -2.5 points a year of Core revenue, and its loops answer (P-05) explains why it persists. - Internal change (C-11 to C-21) credits the new team with cost, tax, financing and governance, and not with revenue. The second set gives high weight to tax, interest and capital expenditure and low weight to revenue (P-06). The lines that drive its EPS growth are the lines the first set said management controls. - Competitive advantage and economic strength (C-41 to C-79) describe a treadmill with strong cash conversion and a low-quality adjusted EPS. The second set models a flat recurring EBITA of about EUR 1.43-1.46 billion, carries restructuring inside earnings and shows both EPS bases. - Risks, guidance and estimates (C-80 onwards) expect a reset of the 2028 targets and further cuts to consensus. The second set's forecasts sit at or below consensus on every line that is defined alike. - The scores are consistent too: the lowest in both sets are for price (C-17: 10; C-45: 12; P-04: 14), the highest for cash, people and the below-the-line items (C-52: 66; C-11: 66; P-08: 52).

  2. The second set already corrected the first on five points, and I agree with four. I agree that FactSet's enterprise value excludes leases; that FactSet's free cash flow line is after dividends and before leases; that the consensus EPS is a blend of definitions; and that the Majorel synergies were not a proven miss (EUR 94 million was booked in 2024 against a EUR 150 million run-rate target for 2025, and Morgan Stanley records a further EUR 20-30 million in 2025, back-end loaded to IT contracts expiring in June). I qualify the fifth, on leverage, in point 3(d).

  3. Seven points where I correct or qualify the earlier sets.

(a) The Trust & Safety base effect arrives in the third quarter of 2027, not the first. The first set (C-06, C-22) expected "a mechanical improvement from Q1 2027" and listed Q1 2027 revenue as "the first quarter lapping the steep declines". The company's own figures say otherwise. Trust & Safety took 3.4 points off group growth in H1 2026 (-1.7% reported, +1.7% excluding it) and 3.2 points in Q3 (-1.2% against +2.0%). Its share of revenue was 6.5% in H1 2026 and 5.5% for nine months, and Morgan Stanley expects about 5% for the year. That implies about EUR 317 million in H1 2026 and only about EUR 90 million a quarter in the second half (my calculation): a second step down in Q3 2026. A second route gives the same figure: the FY2025 share (8.0%) with the H1 2026 drag puts Q3 2025 at about EUR 170 million, and the Q3 2026 drag of 3.2 points takes about EUR 80 million off that. Even if Trust & Safety stops falling at that level, H1 2027 compares about EUR 180 million with EUR 317 million, a drag of about 2.5-3 points; only H2 2027 compares like with like. Revenue prints therefore stay negative through the H1 2027 results in late July 2027, and the first quarter that shows the base effect is Q3 2027, reported in early November 2027.

(b) The second set's half-year shape for FY2027 does not fit its own annual figures. Its Trust & Safety drag for the year (1.4 points) is right on the arithmetic above. But its H1 2027 LFL of -1.5% then needs growth excluding Trust & Safety of about +1.3%, and its H2 2027 of +0.3% needs only about +0.3%. Nothing in the evidence supports a first half that strong. Half of the November guidance cut came from causes other than Trust & Safety (offshoring a quarter; US healthcare volumes and LanguageLine staffing a quarter; Morgan Stanley's account). On my estimate the Trust & Safety drag in Q4 2026 is again about 2.5-3 points, so a quarter at Bernstein's -2.9% or at the midpoint of the guide (-3.2%, Redburn) means that growth excluding Trust & Safety falls from +2.0% in Q3 to about zero; at Deutsche Bank's -1.7% it is about +1%. I use +0.5% for both halves of 2027: LFL of about -2.3% in H1 and +0.3% in H2, about -1.0% for the year. Morgan Stanley and Deutsche Bank are at -1.0% and Bernstein at -1.2%. This is a change of input.

(c) The FY2027 margin of 14.5% assumes that the erosion stops in the second half of 2027, which the second set does not explain. The FY2026 guide implies a second-half recurring margin of about 15.2% against about 15.4% a year earlier, that is EBITA about EUR 15 million lower in spite of savings that the company puts at about half of the run-rate, EUR 75-85 million. The underlying erosion in that one half-year is therefore about EUR 90-100 million, about 1.8% of revenue (my calculation). The programme's help, year on year, is about 1.5 points of revenue in H2 2026 and again in H1 2027, and close to nothing in H2 2027, because by then both halves carry the full run-rate. The second set's 13.9% for H1 2027 and 15.1% for H2 2027 need the erosion to fall from about 1.7 points (H2 2026) to 1.2 points and then to 0.1 point. The fading of Trust & Safety removes part of it. The price give-back, which the second set itself expects to peak in 2027 (P-04), and hub wages do not fade. I use about 13.6% for H1 2027 (flat on H1 2026), about 15.0% for H2 2027 and about 14.3% for the year, and I carry the 0.2 point through to FY2030. Morgan Stanley is at 14.2%, Deutsche Bank at 13.9%, Bernstein and Redburn at 14.5%. The weaker peso (a record 64.20 to the dollar in late 2026) and rupee (down about 7.5% in twelve months) are the main reason I do not go lower: on the first set's sensitivities they are worth up to 0.3-0.5 point of margin before clients and wage inflation take their share. This is a change of input.

(d) Buybacks belong in FY2029, and the leverage comparison with the sell-side was not like for like. The first set expected buyback capacity "from late 2027" on Bernstein's 1.2 times; the second set moved the start to 2028 and said that deleveraging was "slower than the sell-side assumes". Bernstein's 1.7 and 1.2 times are pre-IFRS 16; Morgan Stanley, on the company's basis, has the same 1.8 and 1.5 times as the second set. On our cash flows, starting from the reported EUR 4,013 million at June 2026, net debt is about EUR 3.5 billion at end-2026 (1.8-1.9 times), about 1.6 times at end-2027 and about 1.4 times at end-2028 with no buybacks at all (my calculation). The 1.2 times target, which the chief executive presents as a commercial advantage in vendor consolidation, is not reached before 2029 without a disposal. A board that has bought nothing in 2026 at 4.8 times earnings will not buy EUR 150 million in 2028 while above its own target. Morgan Stanley (2026-11-12) also places buybacks no earlier than FY2029, and notes an 8% French tax on them. I move the start to FY2029 (EUR 200 million), keep EUR 300 million in FY2030, and lower the interest line to match. This matters because buybacks were the largest single source of EPS growth to FY2030 in the second set. It is a change of input.

(e) Restructuring is lower on average, and lumpier, than both sets say. The first set used "about EUR 100 million a year". The second set's series (128, 112-119, 30, 140) came from the gap between two EBITDA bases, which also holds acquisition costs and other items. The company-labelled charges are EUR 13 million, 112 million, 16 million and about 140 million. Two consequences follow. The level in the inputs (EUR 80 million, 60 million, 50 million, 50 million; average 60) is in line with the record (average 70), and a low year after a programme year has a precedent (FY2025 after FY2024). The shape is not in line: the record is of a programme every second year, so a smooth path will be wrong in individual years even if it is right in total. I leave the input unchanged.

(f) The reset may not all come on 2027-02-25 (Reader's key, item 2; I-01).

(g) The impairment trigger is a knife-edge, and a very large charge would not be only cosmetic. The first set said that market value was below book equity; the second said that an impairment "would not affect covenants". At EUR 71.68 the market value is EUR 4.2 billion on 58.4 million basic shares and EUR 4.4 billion on 61.6 million diluted shares, against equity of EUR 4,193 million at June 2026 and a consensus EUR 4,384 million for end-2026. The indicator is met or not met within a few euros of today's price. The disclosed sensitivities point to charges of tens of millions in small units (Health Advocate, PSG, Eastern Europe, Spain), like the EUR 87-97 million of FY2025. A group-wide charge needs the auditors to move the 7.5% post-tax discount rate and the 2.2% terminal growth used for the main units (Morgan Stanley discounts the group's cash flows at 16%). If they did, the minimum-equity covenant leaves headroom of about EUR 1.5 billion, about 35% of goodwill; Concentrix wrote off USD 2.6 billion in two steps. My estimate: a charge of some size on 2027-02-25 is about as likely as not; one above EUR 500 million about one in ten; one that threatens the covenant under one in twenty.

Smaller points. FY2025 LFL growth was +1.3% in the company's results (the first set gave +1.0%). Lease interest was EUR 61 million in FY2024, not about EUR 45 million, so lease principal is nearer EUR 250 million than EUR 260 million; the total is unchanged. Part of the reported decline in Specialized Services (-3.9% in H1 2026) is intercompany elimination under the internal synergy plan, not lost demand: the company says the segment would have grown without it, and Bernstein says LanguageLine is still gaining share.

  1. The model's cross-check: two gaps, both explained, neither showing a wrong input. - Free cash flow. The model is EUR 28-34 million above the second set's figure in every year. This is the "about EUR 30 million a year of cash items the inputs do not capture" that the second set announced. Use the second set's lower figure. For FY2026 even that (EUR 734 million) is above every broker with a stated figure (EUR 654-692 million) and above consensus (EUR 704 million), partly because capital expenditure is EUR 220 million in the inputs against EUR 251 million in consensus. - EPS in FY2029 and FY2030. The model is 2.1% and 3.9% above the second set (15.39 against 15.08; 16.77 against 16.14), while net income agrees within 0.5%. The difference is the share count, and I can reproduce the model's count exactly. It retires shares at EUR 71.68, today's price, in every year, where the second set assumed EUR 75, 80 and 85; and it divides each half-year's profit by the share count at the end of that half-year, where the second set used the year's average. About half of the gap comes from each. No input is wrong. The model's convention raises EPS in buyback years by 2-4%, and the 18-month return is measured on FY2029 EPS. - Net debt (not flagged, but it matters for the leverage the memo quotes). The model shows EUR 3,869 million at end-2026; the second set EUR 3,440 million; my estimate is about EUR 3.5 billion. On my reading the model deducts two dividends in FY2026: its cash flow for the year (212.3) equals free cash flow (766.3) less acquisitions (25) less EUR 529 million, and EUR 262 million was paid in May 2026. In later years it deducts each year's own dividend in that year (FY2027: 792.1 less 40 less the change in net debt of 482.0 leaves 270.1, which is EUR 4.60 on 58.7 million shares). If so, net debt is overstated by one dividend, about EUR 265-285 million, in every forecast year, and leverage by about 0.15 times. EPS and the return are not affected. - The Financial Analyst should check whether the model deducts the FY2026 dividend in the second half of FY2026 on top of the dividend already paid in the reported first half and, if it does, pay each forecast year's dividend in the following year. The Financial Analyst should also say at what share price the model executes buybacks, and whether it can use a rising price.

  2. The one large incongruity: the multiple. The model values the shares at a guideline fair multiple of 18.89 times two-year-forward earnings, reached by 2027-12-31, and so shows a return of +318%, of which 274 points come from the multiple. Nothing in the two sets is congruent with that. The first set judged the fair multiple at 5-7 times until two quarters of positive organic growth are reported (C-50), put a fair trough at 3.5 times (C-83), and recorded a ratings store in which the return rating and the pinned valuation letter "disagree by two grades or more". The shares trade at about 5 times, at the first percentile of their ten-year range. I return to this in the judgement on the fourth set. Here I record only that the model's return is the product of a guideline, not of the forecasts in these sets.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

D4230th of 55

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement. The sell-side narrative articulates almost every dynamic in these sets, and the share price (about five times earnings, the first percentile of ten years) shows that investors have acted on it. The decline of Trust & Safety, the spread of automation into voice, offshoring, price pressure at renewal, restructuring that recurs, deleveraging before buybacks, the dilution from a disposal, the impairment risk, and the dependence of the group margin on Specialized Services are each discussed by at least two of the four houses whose research the AlphaSense reports hold in depth. What is not articulated is narrower: the timing of the Trust & Safety base effect (the third quarter of 2027, not the first); the fading of the savings contribution in the second half of 2027; the fact that FY2027 EPS growth is entirely the fall in restructuring and interest; the blend of definitions inside the consensus EPS; and the minimum-equity covenant. Four of those five are negative for the next six to nine months. For investor commentary, as distinct from the sell-side, the files hold no evidence. I cannot answer that half of the question, and I say so rather than infer it.

Reasoning.

  1. How well known each dynamic is on the sell-side (names and dates from the AlphaSense reports of 2026-11-13).
Dynamic Who discusses it How well known Is our view different?
The Trust & Safety decline and its share of the guidance cut Redburn (2026-11-06); Deutsche Bank and Morgan Stanley (2026-11-05, 2026-11-12); Bernstein (2026-11-09) Fully No
The drag fading as the vertical shrinks Redburn (the decline persists into FY2027); Bernstein (a temporary issue); Morgan Stanley (the remaining work is sensitive) Known as a direction; nobody dates it Yes: not before Q3 2027
Automation spreading to voice (64% of interactions), collections and support Morgan Stanley (headcount -4.1%), Jefferies, Deutsche Bank; DNB Carnegie on collections Fully; the centre of the bear case No; we model it as price, about 2-4% a year, not as a second step
Renewal price pressure Channel checks on Concentrix; Morgan Stanley Known for the industry; not quantified for the company No
Restructuring recurs Deutsche Bank; Morgan Stanley (EUR 50 million a year from FY2027). Against: Bernstein. Redburn models none Debated; in two houses' numbers, absent from two Ours (EUR 80 million, then 60 and 50) is at the cautious end
Savings mostly passed to clients Deutsche Bank ("debate over value capture"); Morgan Stanley Known The fade in H2 2027 is not discussed
Consensus EPS mixes definitions Morgan Stanley noted in February 2026 that estimates differ on restructuring; nobody quantifies it Not known Yes: it is why "beats" and "misses" must be read by broker
FY2027 EPS growth is the fall in restructuring and interest Nobody states it Not known Yes
Deleveraging first; buybacks late Bernstein ("de-leverage focus"); Morgan Stanley (not before FY2029); Deutsche Bank Fully No, after my change
Disposal: value and dilution Bernstein (EUR 2.5 billion at 5.8 times EBITA; about 30% lower EBITA; up to 14% EPS upside with buybacks); Morgan Stanley (sum of the parts +26%; proceeds to debt) Fully; the centre of the bull case No
Impairment risk Morgan Stanley, Deutsche Bank; Redburn shows goodwill above equity Known in general; the unit detail and the covenant are not discussed Slightly: probably small, with a tail that is not trivial
Specialized Services margin, and the group's dependence on its mix Morgan Stanley (since 2025); Bernstein (2026-02-23) Fully No
Lower multiples for language-services assets (AMN, Propio) Jefferies, in research on AMN, not on Teleperformance Partly Yes: a reason to doubt LanguageLine at more than 6-7 times EBITA
Visa outsourcing valued highly (VFS Global at USD 1.9 billion in 2021 and reported at about USD 7 billion in 2024-2025; BLS at a 40% EBITDA margin) Morgan Stanley values TLScontact at 12 times EBIT (EUR 470 million); Bernstein at 6 times (EUR 249 million) Partly A possible positive: the smaller asset may be worth more than the bull case's own arithmetic assumes
Weaker peso and rupee as a cost tailwind in 2027 Redburn mentions currency support; no house quantifies it for 2027 in the reports Little discussed A possible positive: up to 0.3-0.5 point of margin before sharing

  1. The two camps, and where we sit. The bear case is Morgan Stanley's: Underweight since 2026-11-12, a discounted cash flow at a 16% discount rate, and a bear scenario of -3% growth with an 11% margin. The bull case is Bernstein's: Outperform, a free cash flow yield of about 20%, a share price that on its reverse calculation of August 2026 implied revenue falling by more than 20%, and disposals as the catalyst. The two agree on the facts and disagree on the terminal value. Our base case is between them on fundamentals (flat revenue, a held margin, no disposal) and closer to the bears on the next two prints. The mean rating is Hold and the buy share 20%. AlphaSense gives Bernstein's target as EUR 139, which is above the highest target FactSet shows (EUR 105); I could not reconcile the two.

  2. The limits of this evidence. The narrative above rests on four houses (Morgan Stanley, Bernstein, Deutsche Bank, Redburn). The other FactSet contributors (Goldman Sachs, RBC, Oddo BHF, CIC, AlphaValue, TPICAP) are represented only by their numbers, and two of the three highest targets (RBC EUR 105, Oddo EUR 100) belong to houses whose reasoning I have not seen. There is nothing in the files from investors themselves: no fund letters, no social-media commentary, and no positioning beyond what the first set recorded (negligible short interest in the US receipts; puts at EUR 40 to December 2026; the chairman's swap and its probable hedge). The indirect evidence is mixed. The shares rose 52% in nine months against falling estimates, which says that some investors bought the governance and disposal story. They then fell about 15% on the guidance cut, which says that those holders are not patient. I have asked for research on this (the requests file).

  3. Primarily, the next 18 months: is anything positive missing from the narrative? Three things, none of them large. (a) A low restructuring year in FY2027 would lift reported EPS and free cash flow by more than the houses that carry EUR 50 million or more expect, though not by more than Redburn and Bernstein already assume. (b) The currency tailwind on costs. (c) The mechanical step up in growth in the second half of 2027 (about 2.5 points between the two halves on my inputs), which will look like a recovery to anyone who has not dated the base effect. Against them, four things are missing on the negative side (the list in the judgement). The balance for the next six to nine months is negative; for months nine to eighteen it turns neutral to mildly positive.

  4. Secondarily, 18 months to three years. Here the narrative is about terminal value, and our base case is better than what the price implies. A business with flat revenue, a held margin and EUR 750-800 million of free cash flow, against a market value of about EUR 4.2-4.4 billion, earns back its market value in under six years. The price says that investors expect decline or do not believe the earnings. The dynamic that would prove them wrong (growth outside Trust & Safety persisting at +0.5% to +1.5% through a full renewal cycle, with the margin held) is in nobody's narrative as a base case, including the bulls', who rely on a disposal. This is the one place where our assessment is more favourable than the market's, and it cannot be shown to be right before late 2027.

Each layer's own material

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 11 November 2026, 22:22 to 22:45 UK, Opus, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Teleperformance (TEP-FR): first set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

Written 2026-11-11 by the Investment Analyst answering set 1, for the Investment Analyst who builds the operating and financial model (set 2).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reader's key: read this before the answers

Price and size. EUR 71.3 on 2026-11-11 (dashboard; previous close EUR 69.02); EUR 71.68 in the FactSet block. Market capitalisation EUR 4,251 million on 61.6 million diluted shares; enterprise value EUR 7,631 million on FactSet's definition, which includes leases. Net debt at June 2026: EUR 4,013 million on the company's definition (2.1 times EBITDA), EUR 4,020 million on FactSet's lease-inclusive basis (2.29 times).

The one new fact since the memo of 2026-10-11. On 2026-11-05 the company reported third-quarter revenue and cut its FY2026 like-for-like (LFL) revenue guidance to -1.5% to -2.5%, from 0% to 2%. Third-quarter group LFL was -1.2% (Core Services -1.0%, Specialized Services -2.9% in the results table, -2.6% in Redburn's text). Group LFL excluding Trust & Safety (content moderation) was +2.0%. The 14.6% recurring EBITA margin target, the EUR 800-850 million adjusted free cash flow target and restructuring at the high end of EUR 120-140 million were kept. Redburn (2026-11-06) attributes half of the guidance cut to the automation of content moderation by large technology clients, and records Trust & Safety falling from 8.0% of group revenue in FY2025 to 5.5% in the first nine months of FY2026. Morgan Stanley's note title (2026-11-05) says the mid-term guidance is "to be re-visited". The shares fell from about EUR 82 before the release (Redburn's reference price EUR 82.22) to about EUR 69-71.

Bases. Never mix these. Company "recurring EBITA" (FY2025 margin about 14.5-14.6%) is before amortisation of acquired intangibles and before restructuring. FactSet "adjusted EBIT" (FY2025 11.8%) is a different, lower line. Consensus EBIT (FY2026 EUR 1,023 million) is on the brokers' basis. EPS has four bases; for FY2025 the brokers' adjusted figure is EUR 13.20 and reported basic is EUR 8.48. Consensus growth rates in these answers are FactSet's like-for-like figures (brokers' basis on both sides).

Calculations of my own used repeatedly (marked "my calculation" where used).

  1. The FY2026 guidance range, after a nine-month LFL of about -1.5% (Q1 -2.2%, Q2 -1.2%, Q3 -1.2%), implies a fourth quarter between about -1.5% (top of range) and -5.5% (bottom), with the midpoint at about -3.5%.
  2. On an 8% weight, group LFL of -1.2% against +2.0% excluding Trust & Safety implies Trust & Safety revenue falling about 35-40% a year, and Core Services excluding Trust & Safety growing about +2.5% to +3%.
  3. Savings of EUR 150-170 million equal about 1.5-1.7% of revenue; a flat 14.6% margin guide therefore implies that, in 2026, the savings are almost wholly consumed by price give-backs, wage inflation, mix loss and technology cost.
  4. One turn of EV/EBITDA (about EUR 1.93 billion) is about 45% of the market capitalisation: the equity is a leveraged slice of the enterprise.
  5. The market capitalisation (EUR 4,251 million) is now below FactSet's FY2026 book equity (EUR 4,384 million; book value per share EUR 74.57 against a price of EUR 71.3), and that book equity is mostly goodwill.

Dates. Next results (FY2026 and the FY2027 guide): 2027-02-25 in the FactSet block; the memo gave 2027-02-18. Confirm with the company.

Peers supplied. Pernod Ricard (RI-FR), Capgemini (CAP-FR, peer table only) and Rational (RAA-DE) were chosen by script and are not business-model peers. I use them as analogues: Pernod for self-help, leverage and disposals in a structurally questioned category; Rational for what real pricing power looks like; Capgemini as an adjacent IT-services competitor. The true business-model peers in the files are Concentrix, TaskUs, Foundever, Telus Digital, Ibex and Firstsource.

A method caution. The risk reports for Teleperformance, Pernod Ricard and Rational all choose the "False Friend" profile in their competing-hypotheses matrix. Three different businesses giving the same answer points to the method, not to the businesses (the Wisdom Library's "The vaguest explanation wins a fewest-contradictions contest"). I treat "False Friend" as a serious competing hypothesis, not as a finding.

Notes the Investment Analyst wrote inside its groups

Inside the group External change forces (Layer 1)

Horizon: the last 6 months and the next 6 months, compared with the last 12 to 18 months.

Inside the group Internal change forces (Layer 1)

Horizon: the last 6 months and the next 6 months, compared with the last 12 to 18 months.

The Investment Analyst's closing notes

Overall steer for the second set

Teleperformance is a high-cash, no-growth labour business inside a structurally deflating value chain. New management is aligned, candid and cost-disciplined, but has not yet changed the revenue outcome or forecast it reliably. Five things matter most for the operating and financial model:

  1. Revenue in two engines. Group excluding Trust & Safety at +1% to +2%; Trust & Safety down 30-40% a year on a shrinking weight. Group organic about -2% (2026), 0% (2027), +0.5% to +1.5% (2028).
  2. Margin. About one third of gross savings retained. Recurring EBITA 14.4-14.9%. Price give-back in Core Services as the swing factor.
  3. Restructuring as a run-rate line. EUR 80-130 million a year until shown otherwise. EPS shown on both the adjusted and the clean basis.
  4. Below the line. Tax 29-30%, interest falling with deleveraging, a flat-to-rising dividend, no buybacks before late 2027.
  5. The disposal as a separate scenario with explicit inputs (sale multiple, proceeds split, the remaining group's margin), not in the base case.

The single most important date is 2027-02-25.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Teleperformance SE (TEP-FR), the global outsourced customer-experience provider, reporting two segments: Core Services (customer care, technical support, back-office, Trust & Safety content moderation, collections, data services for AI; EUR 8,724 million of FY2025 revenue) and Specialized Services (LanguageLine interpretation, TLScontact visa services, Health Advocate; EUR 1,485 million of FY2025 revenue)

Request: T-P1

Peers:

  • Concentrix (CNXC), the closest scale peer, with a large integration (Webhelp) and two goodwill impairments.
  • TaskUs (TASK), a technology-led operator heavily exposed to content moderation and to one large client.
  • Foundever (private), a sub-scale global peer.
  • TELUS Digital, a mid-tier operator with falling revenue.
  • Ibex (IBEX), which runs autonomous-agent deployments with Sierra.
  • Firstsource Solutions, which is pivoting to outcome-based, software-led delivery.
  • Alorica (private), a US labour-heavy competitor.
  • TTEC, a US operator that was the subject of a take-private attempt.
  • Genpact, a process-outsourcing precedent for transaction-based pricing.
  • Atento, a distressed Latin American operator: the precedent for a cost base that could not be cut fast enough.

Inputs:

  1. The split of fixed and variable costs in a contact-centre outsourcing model. How much of agent labour (personnel is 65-70% of Core Services revenue per Deutsche Bank) falls automatically with volume, given annual agent attrition of 66-68% (Bernstein, FY2024)? How much of site, IT and overhead cost is fixed?
  2. How BPO margins moved with revenue in 2009, in 2020-2021, and in the 2023-2026 slowdown, for Teleperformance and each peer, with dated figures.
  3. Why Teleperformance's Core Services recurring EBITA margin fell from 12.4% in FY2024 to 10.5% in H1 2026 while the group margin held at about 14.6%. How much of the difference was Specialized Services mix (33.0% margin in H1 2026), and how much was cost reduction?
  4. The Future Forward programme: EUR 150-170 million of run-rate savings against EUR 120-140 million of FY2026 restructuring cost, with EUR 109 million booked in H1 2026 (company, 2026-07-30). The FY2026 margin guide held at 14.6%, and Deutsche Bank asks why the savings do not lift the margin. What share of comparable programmes at peers reached reported margins, and over what period?
  5. The track record of announced savings against delivered savings. Majorel synergies were EUR 94 million in 2024 against a EUR 150 million target for end-2025 (Deutsche Bank, 2025-11-14). Concentrix targeted USD 120 million of run-rate Webhelp synergies. Earlier Teleperformance programmes.
  6. Whether "non-recurring" restructuring recurs. The history of restructuring and integration charges at Teleperformance and at peers over five years, and how brokers treat them in adjusted earnings.
  7. Which cost lines have proved cuttable and which have not: - agent labour under hourly or per-seat pricing, against outcome- or transaction-based pricing; - real estate through work from home (about 35-40% of the workforce); - recruitment and training (HaireX, +41% hiring conversion); - European overhead (about 3,300 roles); - exits from low-margin countries.
  8. The size and trend of compute and AI token costs, and of hosting fees paid to Microsoft, AWS and OpenAI, borne by BPOs.
  9. The margin effect of statutory labour changes in Colombia (Law 2466; the 42-hour week from July 2026; a 23% nominal minimum-wage rise for 2026) and of wage-board rises in the Philippines, and how much was recovered through contract clauses.
  10. Whether internal AI productivity gains (20-30% faster resolution, 20-40% less rework, as claimed) reach margin or are passed to clients.

Focus: How much of the EUR 150-170 million of Future Forward savings will stay in Teleperformance's recurring EBITA margin over 2026-2028 once clients take their share of productivity at renewal, and whether restructuring of about EUR 100 million or more a year is structural rather than exceptional. What do peer precedents say about margins when organic revenue is flat to negative for several years?

Request: T-P2

Peers:

  • Concentrix, the closest scale peer, which says it bids selectively on complex work while discounting in rebids.
  • TaskUs, priced largely in US dollars to technology clients.
  • Foundever and TELUS Digital, sub-scale peers described as chasing volume.
  • Ibex and Firstsource, both moving to outcome-based models.
  • Genpact, a precedent for transaction- and outcome-based contracts.
  • AMN Language Services and Propio, US interpretation competitors to LanguageLine.
  • VFS Global and BLS International, visa-services competitors to TLScontact.

Operating conditions:

  1. Contract renewals while generative and agentic AI automates simple customer interactions and content moderation.
  2. Client-led migration of work to lower-cost offshore and nearshore hubs (India, the Philippines, Colombia, Egypt, South Africa).
  3. Statutory and minimum-wage shocks in delivery hubs (Colombia 2026; the Philippine wage boards; India's Labour Codes).
  4. Cyclical cuts in client budgets and volumes (2009; cuts by technology clients in 2023-2025; the 2026 slowdown in IT-services spending).
  5. The move from per-seat and hourly billing to transaction- and outcome-based pricing.
  6. Regulated niches under policy change: US healthcare and legal interpretation under changing immigration policy; government visa contracts at tender.

Inputs:

  1. Evidence of price give-backs at renewal. Expert calls in 2026 cite discounts of 15-25% at renewal and automation discounts of 5-10% on hourly labour. Test how widespread these are, and how they compare with the single-digit adjustments of the past.
  2. The share of enterprise deals with upfront discounting, said to have risen from 10-15% in 2025 to 15-25% by mid-2026 (expert call, 2026-05-06).
  3. Productivity-sharing clauses: how they are written and how often they are triggered.
  4. Cost-of-living and indexation clauses: where they exist (Teleperformance's Latin American operations grew 7% in local currency on such clauses), their lags, and whether they recover statutory costs as well as wage inflation.
  5. The billing-rate gap between onshore and offshore delivery. Bernstein cites 20-30% lower; Morgan Stanley cites India 70% lower than onshore. Also, the annual drag on organic growth: Morgan Stanley cites 150 basis points for Teleperformance; Concentrix guided to 2 percentage points.
  6. Outcome- and transaction-based revenue: over 15% of Teleperformance's revenue in H1 2026, growing mid-single-digit (company). What margins do these contracts earn, and do they hold price better or worse than hourly contracts?
  7. Specialized Services: the margin rose from 30% (FY2024) to 33.0% (H1 2026). How much came from price, and how much from moving LanguageLine delivery onto internal offshore resources? What is LanguageLine's per-minute pricing history, and what is the effect of AI translation on it?
  8. The trend in contract duration (said to be moving from three-to-five-year terms to annual renewals) and its effect on repricing frequency.
  9. Customer concentration and switching costs. The largest client is 6% of FY2025 revenue and the top ten 31% (company). The average tenure of the top 100 clients is 14 years. A switch involves a two-month retraining ramp and six-month compliance audits.
  10. What clients, competitors and brokers say now about pricing in customer-experience outsourcing, and whether price falls when volumes fall.

Focus: Is the net price per unit of work in Teleperformance's Core Services falling, how fast, and under which contract types? Do outcome-based contracts and Specialized Services (LanguageLine in particular) have pricing power that survives AI automation, offshoring and policy change?

Request: T-P3

Why requested: The first set found several self-reinforcing or self-limiting dynamics that will shape Teleperformance's revenue, margin and earnings quality more than any single line item: content-moderation losses that shrink their own weight, savings that are handed back, restructuring that undermines adjusted earnings, and deleveraging that creates buyback capacity.

Peers:

  • Concentrix, which has both an impairment cycle and AI-era contract wins.
  • TaskUs, whose loss of volume at its largest client shows how a single-client automation loop runs.
  • TELUS Digital, a shrinking mid-tier peer.
  • Firstsource and Ibex, outcome-based pivots.
  • Genpact, a longer history of moving to transaction-based pricing.

Inputs:

  1. Trust & Safety automation. Content moderation fell from 8.0% of Teleperformance's revenue in FY2025 to 5.5% in the first nine months of FY2026 and caused half of the November 2026 guidance cut (Rothschild & Co Redburn, 2026-11-06). As its weight shrinks, does the drag on group growth limit itself, or does the same automation spread to other verticals (voice customer care, technical support, collections)?
  2. The savings treadmill. Cost savings are given back at renewal, which forces new restructuring, which keeps adjusted and reported earnings far apart. FY2025 adjusted EPS was EUR 13.20 against reported basic EPS of EUR 8.48 (FactSet).
  3. Attrition and outcome-based contracts. Annual agent attrition of 66-68% erodes the skills needed to hit outcome targets. Missed targets bring penalties and lower margins. Does this loop exist in practice?
  4. Vendor consolidation. Clients cut supplier panels in favour of scale providers. Does that loop raise the share of the largest providers faster than price deflation lowers revenue?
  5. The impairment loop. A low market value leads to goodwill impairment, which reinforces the narrative of value destroyed. Concentrix booked USD 1.52 billion (FY2025) and USD 1.05 billion (Q3 2026); Teleperformance booked EUR 97 million (FY2025), and its market value is near book equity.
  6. Deleveraging and capital return. Lower leverage (Bernstein forecasts 1.7 times at end-2026 and 1.2 times at end-2027) creates capacity to resume buybacks, which raises EPS. Is that a reinforcing loop for the share price?
  7. Offshoring. Volume moves to lower-cost hubs, lowering revenue and raising margin, which funds price concessions, which moves more volume offshore.
  8. AI data services and TP.ai. Over 1,400 projects; 35% of clients (company, 2026-11-05). Is there any evidence that these create a reinforcing loop of client wins?

Focus: Which of these loops will dominate Teleperformance's organic growth and recurring margin in 2027-2028? In particular, does the content-moderation drag fade as its weight shrinks, or does the same automation move into the larger voice and customer-care book?

Request: T-P4

Why requested: The first set found that consensus EPS growth for Teleperformance comes mostly from items below operating profit, and from how restructuring and amortisation are treated, rather than from revenue or margin. The second set needs the evidence on each input to the bridge separately.

Peers:

  • Concentrix, for leverage, refinancing and impairment comparison.
  • Capgemini, an adjacent IT-services company with its own share buybacks and debt.
  • TaskUs, for a capital structure after a take-private attempt.
  • Pernod Ricard, a French large-cap with suspended buybacks while deleveraging from high leverage.
  • Rational, a contrasting case of net cash and a high payout.

Inputs:

  1. Effective tax rate. 33.1% in H1 2025 and 29.0% in H1 2026, guided below 30% for FY2026 as Majorel legal entities merge (company). The reported rate was 36.8% in FY2025 (FactSet). Is the lower rate sustainable?
  2. Net interest. EUR 282 million in FY2025, including leases (FactSet). A EUR 1.2 billion bond in May 2026 extended average maturity to 4.5 years (company). The mix of fixed and floating debt, and the cost of refinancing at current French and euro credit spreads.
  3. Leverage path. 2.0 times at end-2025 and 2.1 times at June 2026 (company); Bernstein forecasts 1.7 times at end-2026 and 1.2 times at end-2027. Free cash flow is about 70% weighted to the second half (Deutsche Bank).
  4. Restructuring charges. EUR 109 million in H1 2026, and EUR 120-140 million for FY2026 at the high end. How do brokers treat them in adjusted EPS? What is the likely level in FY2027, given that mid-term guidance is "to be re-visited" (Morgan Stanley, 2026-11-05)?
  5. Amortisation and impairment. Amortisation of acquired intangibles was EUR 218 million in FY2025 and EUR 106 million in H1 2026. What is the risk of further goodwill impairment while the market value sits near book equity?
  6. Share count and buybacks. EUR 184 million of buybacks in 2024, EUR 114 million in 2025 and none in 2026 (FactSet). The stated policy of returning up to two-thirds of net free cash flow. The conditions for buybacks to resume.
  7. Dividend. EUR 4.50 per share for FY2025 (EUR 262 million paid in H1 2026) and its cover.
  8. The Specialized Services disposal. The possible proceeds (Bernstein: up to EUR 2.5 billion), the multiples paid for comparable interpretation and visa-services businesses, and how proceeds might be split between debt reduction and returns to shareholders.
  9. Currency. Translation cost EUR 165 million of H1 2026 revenue (company); about EUR 20 million of revenue per one-cent move in USD/EUR (Morgan Stanley). The Philippine peso is 25% of cost of goods sold and the Indian rupee 15%. Hedges run up to twelve months.
  10. Recurring EBITA margin. The FY2026 guide is 14.6% at constant currency (company). Analysts' FY2027-2028 expectations, compared with the 2028 target of about 15.5%.

Focus: What does the evidence say about each step of Teleperformance's EPS bridge from the FY2025 adjusted EUR 13.20 to FY2028? The bridge should separate operating contributions (revenue, margin), financing contributions (tax, interest, share count) and definitional ones (the treatment of restructuring and amortisation). Which inputs are most likely to differ from what analysts now assume?

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 12 November 2026, 07:21 to 07:39 UK, Opus, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Teleperformance (TEP-FR): second set of questions, the operating and financial model

Written 2026-11-12 by the Investment Analyst answering set 2, for the Investment Analyst answering sets 3 and 4.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reader's key: read this before the answers

What this set adds. The first set judged Teleperformance a high-cash, no-growth labour business in a value chain whose price per unit of work is falling. This set turns that judgement into numbers, tests the numbers against the company's history and against the four AlphaSense reports written for this set, and states where the first set's figures need correcting.

The base case in one paragraph. Reported revenue of about EUR 9.95 billion in FY2026 (-2.5%), flat in FY2027, then +0.8% and +1.0% a year. Like-for-like (LFL) growth of about -1.9% (FY2026), -0.6% (FY2027) and +0.8% to +1.0% thereafter. Company recurring EBITA margin of 14.4% (FY2026, reported currency), 14.5% (FY2027 and FY2028), easing to 14.3% by FY2030. Restructuring of EUR 140 million (FY2026), 80 million (FY2027), 60 million (FY2028) and 50 million a year after that. Tax at 29.5%. Net financial items falling from EUR 240 million to EUR 185 million. No buybacks until 2028. "Clean" EPS (after restructuring, before amortisation of acquired intangibles) of EUR 12.44, 13.46, 14.19, 15.08 and 16.14 for FY2026 to FY2030. Net free cash flow (company definition, after leases and restructuring) of EUR 734 million, 763 million, 772 million, 786 million and 796 million.

The bases used in this set. They differ from the first set's, so read this.

  1. EBITDA in the forecast inputs is on FactSet's "operating" basis, the basis of the model's own history (FY2025 19.0%, H1 2026 16.0%). I checked that this basis is EBITDA after restructuring: FactSet operating EBITDA plus the year's restructuring reproduces the brokers'-basis EBITDA (FY2024: EUR 1,940 million plus EUR 112 million against EUR 2,059 million; FY2025: EUR 1,943 million plus about EUR 30 million against EUR 1,973 million). My EBITDA therefore deducts restructuring, so that the model's free cash flow is right. It is about 1.0-1.4 points below the brokers' EBITDA margin in FY2026-FY2027 for that reason alone.
  2. Company recurring EBITA (before restructuring and before amortisation of acquired intangibles) is the line management guides on (FY2026: 14.6% at constant currency). My EBITDA equals recurring EBITA, less restructuring, less about EUR 25 million of basis difference between FactSet and the company, plus depreciation and amortisation excluding acquired intangibles (about EUR 490-500 million).
  3. Two EPS lines are carried throughout. "Clean" EPS deducts restructuring and excludes amortisation of acquired intangibles, on FactSet basic shares (58.1 million at FY2025). This is what the model will compute from my inputs. "Adjusted" EPS excludes both restructuring and acquired amortisation, on about 1.3 million more diluted shares; it is the definition Deutsche Bank and Rothschild & Co Redburn use.
  4. The FactSet consensus EPS is a blend of definitions, not one definition (my finding, new in this set). For FY2026, Deutsche Bank (EUR 14.33) and Redburn (EUR 14.35) exclude restructuring; AlphaValue (EUR 12.80) and RBC (EUR 13.42) sit much lower, consistent with deducting at least part of it. The median of EUR 13.46 sits between the two groups. My adjusted EPS (EUR 14.12) matches the first group; my clean EPS (EUR 12.44) is comparable with the second. Any call that consensus will be "beaten" or "missed" must be made broker by broker.
  5. FactSet's historical "free cash flow" is after dividends (my finding). In every year FY2019-FY2025 it equals operating cash flow less capital expenditure less dividends paid (FY2025: 1,431 - 253 - 248 = 930). It is before lease payments, which I estimate at about EUR 305 million a year (principal and interest) from the gap to the company's own net free cash flow (FY2024: 1,609 - 219 - 1,084 = 306). The consensus free cash flow (EUR 704 million, 869 million, 898 million) is on the brokers' basis, close to the company's net free cash flow (after leases, before dividends). The history and the consensus lines are therefore not like for like, although the two differences (leases about EUR 305 million, dividends about EUR 250 million) nearly offset.
  6. FactSet's enterprise value excludes leases (debt of EUR 4,324 million in the snapshot, against total debt of EUR 5,047 million including about EUR 720 million of leases at June 2026). The first set said it included them. Including leases, enterprise value is about EUR 8.35 billion, equity is about 51% of it, and EV/EBITDA on consensus FY2026 is about 4.3 times rather than 4.0 times.

Where I differ from the first set (each is explained in the answer named).

  • FY2027 LFL growth: about -0.6%, not about 0%. Discounting stepped up in mid-2026 and contracts now renew about annually, so the price give-back reaches the revenue line through 2027 (P-04, P-09).
  • Restructuring is lumpy rather than a steady EUR 100 million: about EUR 128 million (FY2023), EUR 112-119 million (FY2024), about EUR 30 million (FY2025) and EUR 140 million (FY2026). The four-year average is still about EUR 100 million, which supports the first set's conclusion that it recurs in substance (P-03, P-08).
  • The like-for-like gap between adjusted and clean EPS for FY2026 is about EUR 1.7-1.9 a share (about 13%), not EUR 1.55 against the consensus median, because the median already deducts part of the restructuring (P-08).
  • The first set's "Majorel synergies at about 75-85% of target" is not established by the sources. They record EUR 94 million in 2024 and "EUR 20-30 million more" in 2025, which could be in-year income-statement savings against a run-rate target of EUR 150 million. The cost-delivery record is no worse than "mostly delivered" (P-06).
  • Leverage on my cash flows reaches about 1.8 times at end-2026 and about 1.5 times at end-2027, not Bernstein's 1.7 times and 1.2 times (P-02).

Dates. FY2026 results and the reset of the mid-term targets: 2027-02-25 (FactSet; the memo gave 2027-02-18). Q1 2027 revenue: late April 2027. H1 2027 results: late July 2027. Q3 2027 revenue: early November 2027.

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Read first: the EBITDA basis. The EBITDA margin in Parts 1 and 3 is on FactSet's "operating" basis, the basis of the model's own history (FY2025 19.0%; H1 2026 16.0%; H2 2025 19.2%). That basis is after restructuring: FactSet operating EBITDA plus the year's restructuring reproduces the brokers'-basis EBITDA (FY2024: 1,940 + 112 against 2,059; FY2025: 1,943 + about 30 against 1,973). I keep restructuring inside EBITDA (EUR 140 million in FY2026, 80 million in FY2027, 60 million in FY2028, 50 million a year after) so that the model's free cash flow deducts it. The model's EPS is therefore a "clean" EPS (after restructuring, before amortisation of acquired intangibles), not the brokers' adjusted EPS. Lease payments in Part 2 are principal only; lease interest (about EUR 45 million a year) is inside net financial items, so that it is not counted twice.

Segments: revenue and margin by segment, where the company reports them

The company reports two segments on recurring EBITA (before restructuring and acquired amortisation). From 2026 AllianceOne and TP Infinity USA moved from Specialized to Core; restated FY2025: Core EUR 8,799 million at 11.9%, Specialized EUR 1,411 million at 31.0% (Bernstein). H1 2026: Core EUR 4,142 million at 10.5% (-50 basis points), Specialized EUR 741 million at 33.0%.

Specialized is about 14% of revenue and about 31% of recurring EBITA. Its H1 2026 margin gain came from cost control, the internal synergy plan (moving LanguageLine work onto Core's offshore delivery) and easy comparisons, not price; I let it ease back. Core's small recovery comes from the end of the high-margin Trust & Safety drag, one third of the 2027 savings and offshoring mix, net of price give-back.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Revenue. Like-for-like growth -1.9% (FY2026: Q1 -2.2%, Q2 -1.2%, Q3 -1.2%, Q4 about -3.0%), -0.6% (FY2027), +0.8% (FY2028), +1.0% (FY2029-FY2030). Trust & Safety (8.0% of revenue in FY2025, 5.5% in the first nine months of 2026) subtracts about 3.1, 1.4, 0.6, 0.4 and 0.4 points. The rest of the group grows about +1.3%, +0.8% and +1.4%: net price including offshoring of about -2 to -2.5 points a year of Core revenue (renewal discounts, automation discounts, offshoring at about -150 basis points a year), against volume from vendor consolidation, back office, outcome-based work and data services of about +2.5-3 points. FY2027 dips because the mid-2026 rise in upfront discounting (deals with upfront discounts rose from 10-15% to 15-25%) reaches revenue through annual renewals. Currency and perimeter: about -0.6 points (FY2026), about +0.6 points (FY2027, as forecasts point to a weaker euro), then zero. Half-years: H2 2026 -0.5% reported (LFL about -2.1%), H1 2027 -0.5% (LFL about -1.5%), H2 2027 +0.5% (LFL about +0.3%). FY2026 revenue of EUR 9,951 million sits just inside the brokers' range (EUR 9,938-10,033 million), which brokers set knowing Q3 reported revenue.
  • Margin. Recurring EBITA 14.4% (FY2026, reported currency, against the 14.6% constant-currency guide), 14.5% (FY2027-FY2028), 14.4% and 14.3%. Future Forward savings of EUR 150-170 million run-rate, half in H2 2026, the rest in 2027, of which I assume one third is retained; the rest goes to clients at renewal and to hub wages (Metro Manila +12.2% in two steps to January 2027; Colombia's 23% minimum-wage rise and 42-hour week). Weaker delivery currencies (the peso is 25% of cost of goods sold, the rupee 15%) help modestly in 2027 as twelve-month hedges roll off. EBITDA (model basis) = recurring EBITA - restructuring - about EUR 25 million of FactSet-to-company basis difference + D&A excluding acquired intangibles.
  • Half-year margins. H2 2026: recurring EBITA about 15.2% (H2 2025 about 15.4%), restructuring about EUR 31 million, giving 19.5%. H1 2027: recurring about 13.9% (H1 2026 13.6%), restructuring about EUR 50 million, giving 17.7%. H2 2027: recurring about 15.1%, restructuring about EUR 30 million, giving 19.1%. The H1 2027 rise from 16.0% is mostly lower restructuring.
  • D&A excluding acquired intangibles. FactSet basis about EUR 245 million in H1 2026; gently falling with low capital expenditure and home working.
  • Net financial items. About EUR 4.3 billion of gross financial debt at an average cost of 4.28% after the May 2026 bond at about 5.0%, about EUR 1.0 billion of cash, lease interest about EUR 45 million and other financial costs. Falls with deleveraging; slowed by refinancing at higher coupons and by buybacks from 2028.
  • Tax. 29.0% in H1 2026, guided below 30% for FY2026 as Majorel entities merge; 29.5% allows for Pillar Two (EUR 4 million in H1 2026) and audit risk.
  • Dividend. Held at EUR 4.50 for FY2026 under the board's debt-first priority and the new framework, then rising about 4% a year; cover about 2.8 times free cash flow.
  • Share count. About +0.3 million a year from performance-share vesting (FY2024 and FY2025 reconcile to buybacks with that much vesting).
  • Capital expenditure. 1.9% of revenue in H1 2026; I let it rise to about 2.4-2.5% because the first set judged part of the fall to be under-investment.
  • Working capital. 16.8% at end-2025 (helped by EUR 110 million of factoring), 17.3% at June 2026; 17.0% at each year end, allowing for client pressure on payment terms.
  • Leases. Total lease cash about EUR 305 million a year (derived from the gap between FactSet operating cash flow less capital expenditure and the company's net free cash flow: FY2024 1,609 - 219 - 1,084 = 306), of which about EUR 260 million principal, falling slowly with home working.
  • Acquisitions. Small AI stakes and bolt-ons only (up to EUR 100 million budgeted for AI partnerships; EUR 2 million in H1 2026). No disposal in the base case.
  • Buybacks. None in FY2026-FY2027 (the board prioritises debt; leverage about 1.8 times at end-2026 and 1.5 times at end-2027 on my cash flows); EUR 150 million, 250 million and 300 million in FY2028-FY2030, under the policy of returning up to two-thirds of net free cash flow, at assumed prices of EUR 75, 80 and 85.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  1. Company recurring EBITA (guided line) excludes restructuring and amortisation of acquired intangibles. FY2025 about EUR 1,480 million (14.5%); my FY2026 EUR 1,433 million (14.4%).
  2. Brokers' EBITDA (the consensus line: FY2026 EUR 1,928 million, 19.3%) is about recurring EBITA plus D&A excluding acquired intangibles, before restructuring. My equivalent is EUR 1,933 million (FY2026), 1,938 million (FY2027) and 1,945 million (FY2028), in line with consensus. The consensus median mixes definitions: AlphaValue (EUR 1,722 million), CIC (1,698 million) and RBC (1,767 million) sit far below the rest.
  3. FactSet operating EBITDA (the model's history and my input basis) = brokers' EBITDA less restructuring (and about EUR 25 million of basis difference): my FY2026 EUR 1,768 million = 1,933 - 140 - 25.
  4. EPS bases. Reported basic EPS deducts restructuring, acquired amortisation (EUR 218 million in FY2025) and impairments (EUR 97 million in FY2025): EUR 8.48 in FY2025; my estimate about EUR 9.7 for FY2026 before any impairment. Clean EPS (the model's basis, my cross-check) deducts restructuring but not acquired amortisation: EUR 12.44 (FY2026). Adjusted EPS excludes both (the Deutsche Bank and Redburn definition), on about 1.3 million more diluted shares: EUR 14.12 (FY2026), 14.41, 14.89, 15.66, 16.73, against Deutsche Bank's 14.33 and Redburn's 14.35 for FY2026. The FactSet consensus median (EUR 13.46, 13.75, 14.22) blends brokers on both definitions; compare it broker by broker.
  5. Free cash flow bases. FactSet's historical free cash flow is operating cash flow less capital expenditure less dividends paid (FY2025: 1,431 - 253 - 248 = 930), and is before lease payments. The company's net free cash flow, and the brokers' consensus (EUR 704 million, 869 million, 898 million), are after lease payments and before dividends. My cross-check is on the company's definition.
  6. Net debt. The company's net debt (EUR 4,013 million at June 2026, 2.1 times) and FactSet's (EUR 4,020 million) both include about EUR 720 million of leases; FactSet's enterprise value (EUR 7,631 million) uses debt of EUR 4,324 million, which excludes them.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: Teleperformance SE (TEP-FR), the global outsourced customer-experience provider; it reports two segments: Core Services (customer care, technical support, back office, Trust & Safety content moderation, collections and data services for AI) and Specialized Services (LanguageLine interpretation, TLScontact visa services and Health Advocate).

Request: T-D1

Peers: Concentrix (CNXC), the closest scale peer, which has delivered Webhelp synergies yet booked two goodwill impairments and seen revenue fall; TaskUs (TASK), a technology-led operator whose largest client automated content moderation; TELUS Digital, a mid-tier operator with falling revenue; Foundever (private), a sub-scale global peer reported to be near a liability management exercise; Ibex (IBEX), a near-fully offshore operator growing double-digit; Firstsource Solutions, about half of whose revenue is outcome-based; Genpact (G), the precedent for moving to transaction- and outcome-based pricing; TTEC, a US operator that was subject to a take-private attempt; Atento, the distressed Latin American precedent; Alorica (private), a US labour-heavy competitor; Pernod Ricard (RI-FR), a French large-cap that suspended buybacks to deleverage while running a cost programme. Operating conditions: contract renewals while generative and agentic AI automates simple interactions and content moderation; client-led migration of work to offshore hubs (India, the Philippines, Egypt, South Africa, Colombia); statutory and minimum-wage shocks in delivery hubs (Colombia 2026, Metro Manila wage boards, India's Labour Codes); cyclical cuts in technology clients' budgets; the shift from hourly and per-seat billing to transaction- and outcome-based pricing; US immigration policy affecting interpretation volumes; high French and euro credit spreads. Inputs: Is it plausible that Teleperformance's organic growth stabilises as Trust & Safety shrinks (8.0% of revenue in FY2025, 5.5% in the first nine months of FY2026, Rothschild & Co Redburn, 2026-11-06), given that group like-for-like growth excluding Trust & Safety was +1.7% in H1 2026 and +2.0% in Q3 2026 (company)? Has any labour-based customer-experience provider kept a material share of cost savings in its margin during several years of flat or falling organic revenue, and how long did it take? What does the record say about the Future Forward programme (EUR 150-170 million of run-rate savings, EUR 120-140 million of FY2026 restructuring, company, 2026-07-30) being followed by lower restructuring rather than a new programme? Has any scale BPO moved a large share of revenue to outcome-based pricing without margin loss, from a starting point of about 15% of revenue (company, H1 2026)? Is the Specialized Services margin of 33.0% in H1 2026 (company) sustainable, given that part of it came from moving LanguageLine delivery onto internal offshore resources? Can a new chief executive without operating experience in the industry (Jorge Amar, from 2026-03-16) deliver a turnaround of this kind, and what distinguished successful from failed turnarounds in labour-based outsourcing? Focus: Whether Teleperformance's expected stabilisation of revenue and margin relies only on strengths it demonstrably has (scale, cash conversion, cost execution, regulated niches) or on strengths it would need to acquire (pricing power in Core Services, retained AI productivity, proprietary technology), judged against how comparable labour-based outsourcers fared in similar transitions.

Request: T-D2

Peers: Concentrix (CNXC), for the dispersion of outcomes during the same AI and offshoring transition; TaskUs (TASK), for a single-client automation step; TELUS Digital, for a shrinking mid-tier operator; Genpact (G) and Firstsource Solutions, for outcome-based transitions; Ibex (IBEX), for an offshore-led operator; Atento and TTEC, for downside precedents; Pernod Ricard (RI-FR), for EPS growth that relied on below-the-line items while operating estimates fell. Operating conditions: AI-driven repricing at renewal with contract terms moving towards annual renewal; accelerated offshoring; step losses of whole client programmes to automation; hub wage inflation and currency moves (Philippine peso, Indian rupee, Colombian peso, US dollar); deleveraging under high credit spreads; a reset of mid-term targets. Inputs: How concentrated are the drivers of Teleperformance's EPS over FY2026-FY2030 among a few items: the fade of the Trust & Safety drag, net price at renewal in Core Services, restructuring charges (EUR 109 million in H1 2026, company), the effective tax rate (33.1% in H1 2025 to 29.0% in H1 2026, company), net interest (EUR 282 million in FY2025, FactSet) and the timing of buybacks (none in 2026 after EUR 114 million in 2025)? How wide has the dispersion of 18-month organic growth and margin outcomes been for labour-based customer-experience providers since 2022, and which events widened it (Klarna's AI assistant on 2024-02-28; Meta's automation at TaskUs; the Trust & Safety decline at Teleperformance)? How often have customer-experience outsourcers' forecasts that rested on one or two drivers (a base effect, a cost programme, buybacks) been met? How large and how frequent have single-client step losses been in the sector (the largest Teleperformance client is 6% of FY2025 revenue and the top ten 31%, company)? What is the historical range of Teleperformance's half-year recurring EBITA margin against initial guidance? Focus: How much of Teleperformance's forecast change from the last four to six years rests on below-operating-profit items (restructuring, tax, interest, share count) rather than on revenue and margin, and how wide and how skewed the range of outcomes over the next 18 months has been in comparable situations.

Request: T-I1

Peers: Concentrix (CNXC), whose results around January 2027 are the next read-across; TaskUs (TASK); TELUS Digital; Ibex (IBEX); Genpact (G); Capgemini (CAP-FR) and Accenture (ACN), for IT-services demand and AI-driven pricing commentary; Firstsource Solutions. Operating conditions: the FY2026 results on 2027-02-25 (FactSet date; confirm against the company's calendar), expected to carry the FY2027 guidance and a revisit of the 2028 targets; Q1 2027 revenue in late April 2027; H1 2027 results in late July 2027; renewal-driven repricing; a possible goodwill impairment test; a possible Specialized Services disposal. Inputs: What FY2027 guidance (like-for-like revenue growth, recurring EBITA margin, restructuring, net free cash flow) do brokers expect at the FY2026 results, and how far are the 2028 targets (4-6% like-for-like growth, about 15.5% recurring EBITA margin, 1.2 times leverage, EUR 3 billion of cumulative 2026-2028 free cash flow, company, June 2025) expected to be cut ("to be re-visited", Morgan Stanley, 2026-11-05)? What does the FY2026 guidance range (-1.5% to -2.5% like-for-like, after Q1 -2.2%, Q2 -1.2%, Q3 -1.2%, company) imply for Q4 2026, and what do brokers model? For each broker in FactSet's consensus, is published adjusted EPS before or after restructuring (FactSet median EUR 13.46 for FY2026 at 2026-11-11; Deutsche Bank EUR 14.33; Rothschild & Co Redburn EUR 14.35; AlphaValue EUR 12.80), and what restructuring do brokers assume for FY2027-FY2028? What free cash flow definitions underlie the consensus of EUR 704 million, 869 million and 898 million for FY2026-FY2028 (FactSet), and how does each broker treat restructuring cash and lease payments? What is the company's record of initial guidance against outcome since 2022, including the July 2026 reconfirmation followed by the November 2026 cut? What do Concentrix's and TaskUs's latest guidance imply for renewal pricing in 2027? Focus: Whether the reset expected at the FY2026 results is likely to land below, on or above current consensus for FY2027, and which consensus lines (revenue, EBITDA, EPS on each broker's definition, free cash flow) are most exposed to revision over the next 18 months.

Request: T-I2

Peers: Concentrix (CNXC); TaskUs (TASK); TELUS Digital; Ibex (IBEX); Genpact (G); Firstsource Solutions; Pernod Ricard (RI-FR), for how the market treated a French large-cap's self-help and deleveraging story. Operating conditions: AI automation and consumer AI agents (Meta's Muse, launched 2026-09-08); offshoring; renewal repricing; the impairment cycle among listed peers; deleveraging and the timing of capital returns; private-market bids for specialised assets. Inputs: the fade of the Trust & Safety drag on group growth as its weight shrinks, against the spread of the same automation into voice customer care, technical support and collections; whether restructuring charges at Teleperformance are recurring rather than exceptional, and how that affects adjusted EPS and free cash flow; the mix of EPS definitions inside FactSet's consensus; the gap between FactSet's historical free cash flow line and the company's net free cash flow; the pace of deleveraging (Bernstein: 1.7 times at end-2026 and 1.2 times at end-2027, 2026-11-09) and when buybacks might resume under the policy of returning up to two-thirds of net free cash flow; the effect on EPS of a Specialized Services disposal under different splits of proceeds between debt and buybacks; the risk of a goodwill impairment while market value (EUR 4,251 million, FactSet, 2026-11-11) sits below forecast book equity (EUR 4,384 million for FY2026, FactSet consensus); the sustainability of the Specialized Services margin; the dependence of the group margin on Specialized Services mix while the Core Services margin fell from 12.4% (FY2024) to 10.5% (H1 2026). Focus: For each of these dynamics, whether the sell-side and investors discuss it prominently, whether only one or two analysts raise it, or whether no one discusses it, with names and dates, so that it is clear which parts of Teleperformance's earnings and cash outlook are already in the narrative and which are not.

Layer 3 and Layer 4: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months / Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

Written 13 November 2026, 10:26 to 10:52 UK, Fable, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Teleperformance (TEP-FR): third and fourth sets of questions, the predictability of the forecasts and what guidance, consensus and the narrative already reflect

Written 2026-11-13 by the Investment Analyst answering sets 3 and 4, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reader's key: read this before the answers

The conclusion in one paragraph. The forecasts for the next 18 months are moderately predictable in their operating lines and poorly predictable in the one line that produces the earnings growth. Revenue and the recurring margin sit inside a narrow band, because the business is diversified, its costs are variable and its largest swing item (Trust & Safety) is now small. But all of the growth in FY2027 earnings per share comes from restructuring charges and interest falling, which the board decides, not the business, and which will be set on one day, 2027-02-25. Guidance, sell-side estimates and the narrative already reflect most of what the first two sets found; where they do not, our view is lower than theirs (FY2027 revenue, free cash flow, EPS after restructuring), not higher. There is no favourable gap for the next six months. One can open only after the reset, and the first hard evidence for it (Trust & Safety lapped, savings visible in the margin) arrives between late July and early November 2027.

What the changes do (my calculations from the model's own arithmetic; the updated model gives the figures of record). Clean EPS of about EUR 12.5 (FY2026, unchanged), 13.1 (FY2027), 13.6 (FY2028), 14.4 (FY2029) and 15.6 (FY2030), against the model's 12.47, 13.42, 14.27, 15.39 and 16.77: lower by 2.5%, 5%, 6% and 7%. Growth of about 5.8% a year, not 7.7%. Adjusted EPS (before restructuring) of about EUR 14.1, 14.0 and 14.3 for FY2026 to FY2028. Net free cash flow on the company's definition of about EUR 734 million, 750 million and 752 million (cumulative FY2026-FY2028 about EUR 2.24 billion). Leverage of about 1.8-1.9 times at end-2026, 1.6 times at end-2027, 1.4 times at end-2028 and 1.2 times at end-2029.

Bases. As in the second set. EPS is "clean" (after restructuring, before amortisation of acquired intangibles, on FactSet basic shares; the model's basis) unless I say "adjusted" (also before restructuring; the definition Deutsche Bank and Rothschild & Co Redburn use). EBITDA margins in the inputs are on FactSet's operating basis, after restructuring. "Recurring EBITA" is the company's guided line, before restructuring. Net free cash flow is the company's definition: after leases, interest, tax and restructuring cash, before dividends. Consensus is the FactSet median of 2026-11-11.

New since the second set was written (2026-11-12), from the AlphaSense reports of 2026-11-13.

  1. Morgan Stanley moved to Underweight on 2026-11-12: FY2027 organic growth -1.0%; recurring EBITA margin 14.5%, 14.2% and 14.1% for FY2026-FY2028; restructuring of EUR 50 million a year from FY2027; adjusted EPS cut 8% on average; leverage 1.8 times (FY2026) and 1.5 times (FY2027); no substantial buybacks before FY2029; any disposal proceeds mainly to debt. The FactSet consensus of 2026-11-11 predates this cut.
  2. Morgan Stanley reports that management will revisit the 2028 targets at a "strategic update in the first half of 2027", and will give the FY2027 restructuring figure with the annual results. The two earlier sets assumed that everything comes on 2027-02-25. It may come in two steps (I-01).
  3. Company-labelled restructuring, reorganisation and synergy costs were EUR 13 million (FY2023), 112 million (FY2024), 16 million (FY2025) and about 140 million (FY2026 guide): an average of about EUR 70 million, in alternating low and high years.
  4. The private placement agreements carry a minimum consolidated equity covenant of EUR 2.69 billion (as stated in the H1 2025 interim report; confirm that it is unchanged), against equity of EUR 4,193 million and goodwill of EUR 4,283 million at June 2026.
  5. Bernstein's leverage of 1.7 times (end-2026) and 1.2 times (end-2027) is on a pre-IFRS 16 basis, excluding leases, not the company's lease-inclusive basis.

Revenue growth (%). FY2027 like-for-like growth is about -1.0%, not -0.6%, because Trust & Safety still takes about 1.4 points off the year and growth in the rest of the group is slowing to about +0.5% (the three brokers that have restated their FY2027 organic growth since the Q3 2026 results are at -1.0% to -1.2%), with currency adding about 0.6 points as before.

EBITDA margin (%). The recurring EBITA margin is about 14.3% in FY2027 to FY2028, 14.2% in FY2029 and 14.1% in FY2030, 0.2 point below the second set in each year, because the year-on-year help from the Future Forward savings ends after H1 2027 while the price give-back at renewal and hub wage inflation continue; restructuring inside EBITDA is unchanged at 140, 80, 60, 50 and 50.

Net financial items. Net debt is lower from FY2028 because buybacks start a year later and are EUR 200 million smaller in total (the Part 2 change), which saves interest at about 4.5%.

Buybacks less new shares issued for cash. On these cash flows leverage on the company's definition is still about 1.4 times at end-2028, above the 1.2 times target that the board has put ahead of buybacks, so the first buyback falls in FY2029, as Morgan Stanley (2026-11-12) also expects.

Revenue growth (%). Trust & Safety revenue stepped down again in Q3 2026 (about EUR 317 million in H1 2026 against about EUR 90 million a quarter in H2 2026), so it still takes about 2.8 points off growth in H1 2027 and nothing in H2 2027: like-for-like growth is about -2.3% in H1 2027 and about +0.3% in H2 2027, with currency adding about 1.0 and 0.2 points.

EBITDA margin (%). The recurring EBITA margin is about 13.6% in H1 2027 (flat on H1 2026, with restructuring of about EUR 50 million) and about 15.0% in H2 2027 (against about 15.2% in H2 2026, with restructuring of about EUR 30 million), because the FY2026 guide itself implies underlying erosion of about 1.7 points a year that the savings only just cover.

Co-movement of TEP-FR with its dashboard peers (computed 2026-11-12 from the master dashboard's daily prices)

What this is: daily close-to-close returns over rolling windows of 126 trading days (about six months). Correlation: how closely the daily moves line up (1 = always together, 0 = unrelated). Beta: how far the stock moves for a 1% move in the basket. R-squared: the share of the stock's daily price variance the peer basket explains; the remainder is the stock's own (idiosyncratic) movement. Basket: the peers below, equal-weighted. Prices only; the multiple's own history is in the financials block.
